satisfies a Bernstein’s inequality on its Taylor coefficients over the interval
{\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda}
. Namely,
indicating by

f_{j}(t)=\sum_{\beta=0}^{+\infty}a_{j\beta}(u)\,t^{\beta}\quad,\qquad j\in\{1,...,m\}\ ,\ \ j\neq i\ ,

the Taylor expansion of
f_{j}
at some point
u\in{\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda}
, there exists a positive constants
K_{2}(r,n,m)
, and
{\mathtt{M}}={\mathtt{M}}(r,n,m,\beta):=\beta!\times({\mathtt{K}}(r,n,m))^{\beta}\times K_{2}(r,n,m)
for which the following uniform estimate holds:

\max_{u\in{\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda}}|a_{j\beta}(u)|\leq\frac{{\mathtt{M}}}{\lambda^{\beta-1}}\ .

(4.1)

Remark 4.1
.

The Theorem above corresponds to reasonings holding in the polynomial setting. Moreover, the constants
{\mathtt{d}},{\mathtt{K}},{\mathtt{M}}
depend only on the degree of the considered polynomial. Consequently, this Theorem holds uniformly for any
r
-jet of any function
h\in C^{r}(B^{n}(0,2\delta),\mathbb{R})
.

Remark 4.2
.

Nekhoroshev calls
\gamma
"minimal arc with uniform characteristics" (see
[
47
]
, section 4). In that work, the statement of Theorem
4.1
is not given in the form above but is rather split in dispersed parts. Moreover, many of the modern tools of real-algebraic geometry were lacking at that time, so that the redaction of his work appears quite obscure in some parts. These two elements makes difficult for the reader to reconstruct simply Theorem
4.1
from Nekhoroshev’s original paper.

Remark 4.3
.

The Bernstein’s inequality at point 5 of Theorem
4.1
is essential in order to have stable
12
12

12

In the sense given in Th.
Theorem
, that is valid for an open set of functions.
lower estimates for the steepness coefficients of
h
. For more details about this result, which is interesting in itself and has applications in various fields of mathematics, see refs.
[
55
]
and
[
13
]
.

Some intermediate Lemmas are needed before demonstrating Theorem
4.1
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Consider any triplet of integers
r,n\geq 2
and
m\in\{1,...,n-1\}
. There exists
\mathsf{d}=\mathsf{d}(r,n,m)\geq 0
such that, for any
Q\in\mathcal{P}(r,n)
verifying
\nabla Q(0)\neq 0
, and for any subspace
\Gamma^{m}
perpendicular to
\nabla Q(0)
, the thalweg
\mathcal{T}(Q,\Gamma^{m})
is a semi-algebraic set satisfying
{\rm diag}(\mathcal{T}(Q,\Gamma^{m}))\leq\mathsf{d}
(see Def.
A.2
). Moreover, for any fixed
\eta_{0}>0
, the intersection of
\mathcal{T}(Q,\Gamma^{m})
with the sphere
{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta_{0}}\subset\Gamma^{m}
is compact.

Proof.

\Gamma^{m}
is obviously isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
with the metric induced by the euclidean ambient space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and thus admits a global system of orthonormal coordinates
x=(x_{1},...,x_{m})
. We denote by
P(x)\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
the restriction of
Q(I)
to
\Gamma^{m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Since we endow
\Gamma^{m}
with the induced euclidean metric, studying the norm of the projection of
\nabla_{I}Q(I)
on
\Gamma^{m}
amounts to studying the induced norm of
\nabla_{x}P(x)
on
\Gamma^{m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Now, consider the semi-algebraic set

{\mathcal{E}}:=\{(x,y,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2m}\times\mathbb{R}:||x||_{2}^{2}=||y||_{2}^{2}=\eta^{2},\eta>0,||\nabla P(x)||_{2}>||\nabla P(y)||_{2}\}

(4.2)

By the Theorem of Tarski and Seidenberg
A.1
and Proposition
A.1
, we have that the set

\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\pi_{x}{\mathcal{E}}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}:\forall(y,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R},(x,y,\eta)\not\in{\mathcal{E}}\}

is semi-algebraic. We claim that it coincides with
\mathcal{T}(Q,\Gamma^{m})
. Infact, by the definition of
{\mathcal{E}}
, it is clear that for any given
\eta>0
one has

x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\pi_{x}{\mathcal{E}}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ ||\nabla P(x)||_{2}\leq||\nabla P(y)||_{2}\text{ for all }y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ s.t. }{||y||_{2}}^{2}={||x||_{2}}^{2}\ ,

(4.3)

so that
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\pi_{x}{\mathcal{E}}
is the locus of minima on any given sphere for
||\nabla P||_{2}
(that is for
||\pi_{\Gamma^{m}}\nabla Q||_{2}
), that is it coincides with the Thalweg
\mathcal{T}(Q,\Gamma^{m})
. Moreover, since
\deg P\leq r
, the diagram of
{\mathcal{E}}
is uniformly bounded w.r.t. any
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
and, again by the Theorem of Tarski and Seidenberg, the same is true for
\pi_{x}{\mathcal{E}}
and for
\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\pi_{x}{\mathcal{E}}\equiv\mathcal{T}(Q,\Gamma^{m})
.

It remains to prove that
\mathcal{T}(Q,\Gamma^{m})\cap{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta_{0}}
is compact. By construction,
\mathcal{T}(Q,\Gamma^{m})\cap{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta_{0}}
is the locus of minima of
||\nabla P||_{2}
on
{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta_{0}}
. Since the restriction of the function
||\nabla P||_{2}
to
{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta_{0}}
is continuous for the topology induced by
\Gamma^{m}
, the inverse image of its minimal value on
{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta_{0}}
is closed. Since
{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta_{0}}
is compact, the thesis follows. ∎

The next Lemma shows how an analytic curve with uniform characteristics can be extracted from the Thalweg.

Lemma 4.2
.

Fix a triplet of integers
r,n\geq 2
and
m\in\{1,..,n-1\}
. There exist positive constants
D(r,n,m)\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
K_{i}=K_{i}(r,n,m)\in\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,2
, such that, for any
\xi>0
, for any polynomial
Q\in\mathcal{P}(r,n)
satisfying
\nabla Q(0)\neq 0
, and for any
m
-dimensional subspace
\Gamma^{m}
orthogonal to
\nabla Q(0)
, there exists a semi-algebraic curve
\phi=(\phi_{1}(\eta),...,\phi_{m}(\eta)):[0,\xi]\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}(Q,\Gamma^{m})
having the following properties

1.

For any fixed
\eta\in[0,\xi]
, the intersection
\text{ Im}(\phi)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\eta}^{m}
is a singleton;

2.

The diagram of
\text{graph}(\phi)
(see Definition
A.2
) is bounded by
D
;

3.

There exists a closed interval
{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}\subset[0,\xi]
of length
\xi/K_{1}
over which
\phi
is real-analytic, with complex analyticity width
\xi/K_{1}
;

4.

On the closed complex polydisk
({\mathcal{I}}_{\xi})_{\xi/K_{1}}
of width
\xi/K_{1}
around
{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}
, one has the uniform Bernstein’s inequality

\max_{\eta\in({\mathcal{I}}_{\xi})_{\xi/K_{1}}}|\phi_{j}(\eta)|\leq K_{2}\,\xi\qquad\text{ for any $j\in\{1,...,m\}$.}

Proof.

As in Lemma
4.1
, we consider the isomorphism
\Gamma^{m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and we endow
\Gamma^{m}
with a global system of orthonormal coordinates
x=(x_{1},...,x_{m})
for the scalar product induced by the ambient space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We proceed by steps. At the first step, we build a semi-algebraic function
\phi_{1}
associating to a sphere
{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta}
of given radius
\eta>0
the minimal value attained by the coordinate
x_{1}
on
{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta}
. At Step 2 we apply Yomdin’s reparametrization Lemma (see appendix
B
) to one of the algebraic components of
\phi_{1}
and we get a function with the suitable properties. Finally, at Step 3, we repeat the same construction for the other coordinates.

Step 1.

For any
\xi>0
, by Lemma
4.1
the set

\mathcal{T}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m}):=\mathcal{T}(Q,\Gamma^{m})\cap\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:||x||_{2}^{2}\leq\xi^{2}\}

is semi-algebraic and its diagram is bounded by a positive constant
\mathsf{d}=\mathsf{d}(r,n,m)
. By the Theorem of Tarski and Seidenberg
A.1
, the continuous function

f_{1}:=\mathcal{T}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\quad,\qquad x\longmapsto x_{1}

is semi-algebraic and its diagram is bounded by a quantity depending only on
r,n,m
. Infact,

\text{graph}(f_{1}):=\Pi_{\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}}\{(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\ |\ u\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m}),u=v\}\ .

Moreover, Lemma
4.1
ensures that for any
0<\eta_{0}<\xi
the set
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m})\cap{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta_{0}}
is compact, so that
f_{1}
has a minimum on it. On the other hand, the function
g_{1}:\mathcal{T}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
x\longmapsto||x||_{2}
is also semi-algebraic and its diagram is bounded by a quantity depending only on
r,n,m
, because

\text{graph}(g_{1}):=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}\ |\ x\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m})\ ,\ \ ||x||_{2}^{2}-y^{2}=0\}\ .

Then, by applying Proposition
A.11
, we have that the function

\phi_{1}:[0,\xi]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad\eta\longmapsto\inf_{x\in g_{1}^{-1}(\eta)}f_{1}(x)=\min_{x\in\mathcal{T}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m})\cap{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta}}\{x_{1}\}

(4.4)

is semi-algebraic and we indicate by
d_{1}=d_{1}(r,n,m)
its diagram.

Step 2.
Corollary
A.1
ensures the existence of a number
N_{1}=N_{1}(d_{1})
and of an open interval
{\mathcal{I}}^{1}\subset[0,\xi]
of length
\xi/N_{1}
over which the restriction
\phi_{1}|_{{\mathcal{I}}^{1}}
is algebraic. By Proposition
A.6
,
\phi_{1}|_{{\mathcal{I}}^{1}}
is
d_{1}
-valent and has no more than
d_{1}
zeros on its domain so that there exists an interval
{\mathcal{J}}^{1}\subset{\mathcal{I}}^{1}
of length
\xi/(N_{1}(d_{1})\times(d_{1}+1))
over which the restriction
\phi_{1}|_{{\mathcal{J}}^{1}}
has definite sign. Without loss of generality we can assume
\phi_{1}(\eta)\geq 0
for all
\eta\in{\mathcal{J}}^{1}
(one considers
-\phi_{1}
otherwise).

We denote by
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
the extremal points of the interval
{\mathcal{J}}^{1}
and we rescale the domain by setting

\varphi_{1}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\quad,\qquad\varphi_{1}(u):=\phi_{1}\left(\frac{u+1}{2}\xi_{2}-\frac{u-1}{2}\xi_{1}\right)\ .

(4.5)

We also define the function

\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(u):=\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{1}(u)}{\xi}\quad,\qquad 0\leq\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(u)\leq 1\ ,

(4.6)

which satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem
B.1
. With the notations of Theorem
B.1
, we choose the value
\delta:=\displaystyle\frac{1}{8{\mathtt{Y}}_{1}(d_{1})}
so that, once at most
{\mathtt{Y}}_{1}
neighborhoods of length
2\delta
around the singularities of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}
are eliminated from
[-1,1]
, the remaining set has a measure which is no less than

2-\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{{\mathtt{Y}}_{1}}2\times\displaystyle 1/(8{\mathtt{Y}}_{1})=7/4\ .

Moreover, the number of the partition intervals is bounded by the uniform quantity
{\mathtt{Y}}_{2}\log_{2}\left(8{\mathtt{Y}}_{1}\right)
, so that there exists an interval
\Delta_{1}\subset[-1,1]
verifying

|\Delta_{1}|=\frac{7}{4\,{\mathtt{Y}}_{2}\log_{2}\left(8{\mathtt{Y}}_{1}\right)}

on which
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}
is real-analytic with uniform analyticity width
\frac{3}{2}|\Delta_{1}|
. Infact, by Proposition
B.1
, the complex singularities of
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}
are at distance no less than
3|\Delta_{1}|
from the center
c_{1}
of
\Delta_{1}
. By Theorem
B.1
,
\Delta_{1}
can be affinely reparametrized by a function
\psi_{1}:[-1,1]\longrightarrow\Delta_{1}
, which maps the closed complex disc
\overline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{3}(0)
into the closed complex disc
\overline{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\varrho}(c_{1})
of radius
\varrho:=\frac{3}{2}|\Delta_{1}|
. Hence, we can write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\max_{u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\varrho}(c_{1})}|\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(u)|&\leq\max_{u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\varrho}(c_{1})}|\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(u)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(0)|+|\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(0)|\\
&=\max_{z\in{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(0)}|\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}\circ\psi_{1}(z)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}\circ\psi_{1}(0)|+|\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(0)|\\
\end{split}

(4.7)

so that, by Definition
B.1
and Theorem
B.1
and by the fact that
|\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(u)|\leq 1
for any
u\in[-1,1]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\max_{u\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\varrho}(c_{1})}|\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}(u)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\ .

(4.8)

Scaling back to the original variables, by (
4.5
) the interval
\Delta_{1}
is mapped into an interval
\Delta_{1}^{\xi}
of length
|\Delta_{1}^{\xi}|=|\Delta_{1}|\frac{\xi_{2}-\xi_{1}}{2}=|\Delta_{1}|\frac{\xi}{2N_{1}(d_{1})\times(d_{1}+1)}
and center
\widehat{c}_{1}
and, in the same way, the radius rescales as
\varrho\rightsquigarrow\varrho\frac{\xi}{2N_{1}(d_{1})\times(d_{1}+1)}
. Therefore, taking into account (
4.6
) and (
4.8
), there exists a uniform constant
{\mathtt{M}}_{1}={\mathtt{M}}_{1}(d_{1})
such that the following Bernstein’s inequality is satisfied

\max_{z\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\xi/{\mathtt{M}}_{1}}(\widehat{c}_{1})}|\phi_{1}(z)|\leq 2\xi\ .

(4.9)

Step 3.
Since
\Delta_{1}^{\xi}
is compact,
\phi_{1}(\Delta_{1}^{\xi})
is also compact and the inverse image
\mathtt{U}^{1}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m}):=g_{1}^{-1}(\phi_{1}^{-1}(\phi_{1}(\Delta_{1}^{\xi})))
is closed. Moreover, since the diagrams of
\phi_{1}
and
g_{1}
depend only on
r,n,m
, then by Propositions
A.7
-
A.8
-
A.9
the diagram of
\mathtt{U}^{1}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m})
also depends only on
r,n,m
. Hence, for any fixed
\eta\in\Delta_{1}^{\xi}
we have that the set
{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta}\cap\mathtt{U}^{1}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m})
, which contains the points of the Thalweg that have minimal coordinate
x_{1}
on the sphere of radius
\eta
, is compact and semialgebraic with a bound on its diagram depending only on
r,n,m
. Hence, the coordinate
x_{2}
admits a minimum on this set and we can repeat the same argument of Step 2 on the function

\phi_{2}:\Delta_{1}^{\xi}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\qquad\eta\longmapsto\min_{x\in g_{2}^{-1}(\eta)}f_{2}(x)=\min_{x\in{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta}\cap\mathtt{U}^{1}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m})}\{x_{2}\}

(4.10)

where we have set

f_{2}:{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta_{0}}\cap\mathtt{U}^{1}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\ ,\ \ x\longmapsto x_{2}\quad
and

g_{2}:{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta_{0}}\cap\mathtt{U}^{1}_{\xi}(Q,\Gamma^{m})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},x\longmapsto||x||_{2}\ .

The curve
\phi:=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},...,\phi_{m})
is constructed by iterating this procedure
m
times.

Points 1, 2, and 3 of the thesis follows easily from this construction. Point 4 is a consequence of estimate (
4.9
) applied to the complex polydisk of uniform width
\xi/K_{1}
around the common uniform real interval of analyticity
{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}
of the functions
\phi_{1},...,\phi_{m}
.

∎

We are now ready to state the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Proof.

(Theorem
4.1
)
We assume the setting of Lemma
4.2
with
Q
equal to the Taylor expansion
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)
, and we proceed by steps. At Step 1, we show that there exists a component
\phi_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, of the curve
\phi
introduced in Lemma
4.2
whose first derivative admits a lower bound on a domain of uniform length. Then, at the second step, we use this fact to apply a quantitative inverse function Theorem and we reparametrize
\phi
by the
i
-th coordinate. Steps
3
and
4
contain, respectively, the proofs of points 1-4 and of point 5 in the statement.

Step 1.
We cut the uniform interval of analyticity
{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}
into three equal intervals and we denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}
the central one, whose length is
|\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}|=|{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}|/3
. We indicate by
\widehat{\xi}_{1}
and
\widehat{\xi}_{2}
the extreme points of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}
. Since for any given
\eta\in\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}
by Lemma
4.2
we have
\eta^{2}=\phi_{1}^{2}(\eta)+...+\phi_{m}^{2}(\eta)
, there must be some component
\phi_{i}
of the curve, with
i\in\{1,...,m\}
, verifying

|\phi_{i}(\widehat{\xi}_{2})-\phi_{i}(\widehat{\xi}_{1})|\geq\frac{|\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}|}{m}=\frac{\xi}{3mK_{1}}\ .

(4.11)

At the same time, for some point
\widehat{\xi}_{3}\in\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}
we have

|\phi_{i}(\widehat{\xi}_{2})-\phi_{i}(\widehat{\xi}_{1})|=|\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi}_{3})||\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}|=|\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi}_{3})|\frac{\xi}{3K_{1}}\ .

(4.12)

On the one hand, relations (
4.11
) and (
4.12
) together imply

|\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi}_{3})|\geq\frac{1}{m}\ .

(4.13)

On the other hand, for any
\eta\in[\widehat{\xi}_{3}-|\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}|,\widehat{\xi}_{3}+|\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}|]\subset{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}
one has the estimate

|\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\eta)-\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi}_{3})|\leq\max_{{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}}|\phi^{\prime\prime}_{i}||\eta-\widehat{\xi}_{3}|

(4.14)

which, thanks to the classic Cauchy estimate and to the Bernstein inequality of Lemma
4.2
, implies

|\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\eta)-\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi}_{3})|\leq\frac{2K_{1}^{2}}{\xi^{2}}\max_{({\mathcal{I}}_{\xi})_{\xi/K_{1}}}|\phi_{i}||\eta-\widehat{\xi}_{3}|\leq\frac{2K_{1}^{2}K_{2}}{\xi}|\eta-\widehat{\xi}_{3}|\ .

(4.15)

Hence, for any
\eta
in the interval
J_{\xi}:=\left[\widehat{\xi}_{3}-\displaystyle\frac{\xi}{4mK_{1}^{2}K_{2}},\widehat{\xi}_{3}+\displaystyle\frac{\xi}{4mK_{1}^{2}K_{2}}\right]\subset{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}
we have by (
4.13
) and (
4.15
) that

|\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\eta)|\geq\left||\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi}_{3})|-|\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\eta)-\phi_{i}^{\prime}(\widehat{\xi}_{3})|\right|\geq\frac{1}{m}-\frac{2K_{1}^{2}K_{2}}{\xi}\frac{\xi}{4mK_{1}^{2}K_{2}}=\frac{1}{2m}\ .

(4.16)

Step 2.
By Lemma
4.2
and by the construction at Step 1 we can apply the quantitative local inversion Theorem
C.2
for
\phi_{i}
at any point
\eta\in J_{\xi}\subset{\mathcal{I}}_{\xi}
. By making use of the notations in Theorem
C.2
, we can set the uniform parameters

R:=\frac{\xi}{K_{1}}\quad,\qquad|\phi^{\prime}_{i}(\eta)|\geq\frac{1}{2m}\quad,\qquad\max_{(J_{\xi})_{\xi/K_{1}}}|\phi_{j}^{\prime\prime}|\leq 2\frac{K_{1}^{2}K_{2}}{\xi}\ .

(4.17)

Hence,
\phi_{i}
is invertible in the complex closed polydisk
(J_{\xi})_{R^{\prime}/16}
around the real interval
J_{\xi}
, where

R^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{2}\times\min\left\{R,\displaystyle\frac{\min_{J_{\xi}}|\phi_{i}^{\prime}|}{\max_{(J_{\xi})_{\xi/(2K_{1})}}|\phi_{i}^{\prime\prime}|},\right\}=\frac{\xi}{8mK_{1}^{2}K_{2}}\ .

Since, by construction,
\phi_{i}
is real-analytic in
J_{\xi}
, the continuity of the derivative ensures that
\phi_{i}(J_{\xi})
is an interval of
\mathbb{R}
and, by the definition of the function
\phi_{i}
in (
4.4
), one has
\phi_{i}(J_{\xi})\subset[-\xi,\xi]
. The inverse function is analytic in the complex polydisc of uniform width

R^{\prime\prime}:=\min_{J_{\xi}}|\phi_{i}^{\prime}|\frac{R^{\prime}}{8}\geq\frac{R^{\prime}}{16m}=\frac{\xi}{128\,m^{2}K_{1}^{2}K_{2}}

(4.18)

around
\phi_{i}(J_{\xi})
. Moreover, using (
4.16
), one has that

|\phi_{i}(J_{\xi})|\geq\min_{J_{\xi}}|\phi_{i}^{\prime}|\times|J_{\xi}|\geq\frac{1}{2m}\times\frac{\xi}{2mK_{1}^{2}K_{2}}\ .

(4.19)

Step 3.
Point 1 of Theorem
4.1
follows by Point 1 of Lemma
4.2
and by the local inversion Theorem applied at Step 2. Points 2, 3, and 4 of Theorem
4.1
are also immediate consequences of the local inversion Theorem at Step 2.

Namely, by keeping in mind the notations at Point 4 of Theorem
4.1
, the curve
\gamma:=\phi\circ\phi_{i}^{-1}
can be defined as

\gamma(t):=\begin{cases}x_{i}=t\\
x_{j}(t)=f_{j}(x_{i}):=\phi_{j}(\phi_{i}^{-1}(x_{i}))\qquad\text{ for all }j\in\{1,...,m\}\,,\quad j\neq i\ .\end{cases}

(4.20)

The existence of an interval of analyticity with uniform length and complex width for
\gamma
is a consequence of (
4.18
) and (
4.19
) and the constant
{\mathtt{K}}
in the statement can be taken equal to

{\mathtt{K}}:=128\,m^{2}K_{1}^{2}K_{2}\ .

(4.21)

Indeed, for any
0<\lambda\leq\xi
,
{\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda}
can be chosen to be any interval of length
\lambda/{\mathtt{K}}
contained in the interval
\phi_{i}(J_{\lambda})\subset[-\lambda,\lambda]
(see (
4.19
)). For later convenience, we also observe that the above discussion implies that

({\mathcal{I}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda/K_{1}}\supset(J_{\lambda})_{\lambda/K_{1}}\supset\phi_{i}^{-1}({\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda/{\mathtt{K}}}\quad,\qquad\forall\lambda\in(0,\xi]\ .

(4.22)

The fact that the diagram of
\text{graph}(\gamma)
depends only on
r,n,m
is an immediate consequence of (
4.20
), together with point 2 of Lemma
4.2
and with Propositions
A.8
-
A.9
.

Step 4.
It remains to prove the Bernstein’s inequality at Point 5 of the statement.
By Lemma
4.2
, for any
\lambda>0
we have

\max_{\eta\in({\mathcal{I}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda/K_{1}}}|\phi_{j}(\eta)|\leq K_{2}\,\lambda

(4.23)

for some uniform constant
K_{2}=K_{2}(r,n,m)
and for any
j\in\{1,...,m\}
. By construction in (
4.20
),

f_{j}(x_{1})=\phi_{j}\circ\phi_{i}^{-1}(x_{i})
, and for any
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
the classic Cauchy estimate implies

\max_{t\in{\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda}}|f_{j}^{(\beta)}(t)|\leq\beta!\,{\mathtt{K}}^{\beta}\frac{\max_{z\in({\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda/{\mathtt{K}}}}|f_{j}(z)|}{\lambda^{\beta}}=\beta!\,{\mathtt{K}}^{\beta}\frac{\max_{z\in({\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda/{\mathtt{K}}}}|\phi_{j}\circ\phi_{i}^{-1}(z)|}{\lambda^{\beta}}\ .

(4.24)

For any
0<\lambda\leq\xi
, by (
4.22
) one has
\phi_{i}^{-1}(({\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda/{\mathtt{K}}})\subset({\mathcal{I}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda/K_{1}}
. Taking this into account, (
4.24
) and (
4.23
) yield

\max_{t\in{\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda}}|f_{j}^{(\beta)}(t)|\leq\beta!\,{\mathtt{K}}^{\beta}\frac{\max_{\eta\in({\mathcal{I}}_{\lambda})_{\lambda/K_{1}}}|\phi_{j}(\eta)|}{\lambda^{\beta}}\leq\beta!\,{\mathtt{K}}^{\beta}\frac{K_{2}\lambda}{\lambda^{\beta}}=\beta!\,{\mathtt{K}}^{\beta}\frac{K_{2}}{\lambda^{\beta-1}}\ .

(4.25)

The thesis at Point 5 in the statement follows by setting
{\mathtt{M}}=\beta!\times K_{2}\times{\mathtt{K}}^{\beta}
.

∎

5
s
-vanishing polynomials

We take into account the results and the notations of the previous section, in particular Theorem
4.1
.

5.1
Heuristics and Definitions

The goal of the first part of this paragraph is to provide the reader with a heuristic justification for introducing the special class of
s
-vanishing polynomials in the study of the genericity of steepness. A rigorous description of the rôle played by these polynomials will be given in the next paragraphs and sections.

For any fixed integer
n\geq 2
, we consider the euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and we endow any of its linear subspaces with the induced metric. For any pair of positive integers
1\leq m\leq n-1
and
r\geq 2
, for any given function
h
of class
C^{r}
near the origin verifying
\nabla h(0)\neq 0
, and for any
m
-dimensional subspace
\Gamma^{m}
orthogonal to
\nabla h(0)
, by Def.
4.1
, the set
\mathcal{T}({\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n),\Gamma^{m})
is the locus of minima of

||\pi_{\Gamma^{m}}\nabla{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)||_{2}
on the spheres
{\mathcal{S}}^{m}_{\eta}(0)\subset\Gamma^{m}
, with
\eta>0
.

In Theorem
4.1
we have proved the existence of a minimal semi-algebraic arc
\gamma
(see (
4.20
)) of diagram
{\mathtt{d}}(r,n,m)
parametrized by one coordinate and whose image is contained in the thalweg
\mathcal{T}({\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n),\Gamma^{m})
. Due to Proposition
A.4
-
\gamma
is piecewise algebraic, with a maximal number of algebraic components depending only on its diagram
{\mathtt{d}}(r,n,m)
. With the exception of a finite set of complex points, any algebraic function admits a local holomorphic extension, and the number of its singularities is bounded by a quantity depending only on its diagram (see appendix
B
, or
[
13
]
for more details). Therefore,
\gamma(t)
is real-analytic with the exception of a finite number of points whose cardinality is bounded uniformly by a quantity depending solely on
{\mathtt{d}}(r,n,m)
. In particular, for any
\lambda>0
, this ensures the existence of an interval
{\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda}\subset[-\lambda,\lambda]
of uniform length
\lambda/{\mathtt{K}}(r,m,n)
, where
{\mathtt{K}}={\mathtt{K}}(r,m,n)
is a suitable constant, over which
\gamma(t)
is real-analytic with complex analyticity width
\lambda/{\mathtt{K}}
.

By the above reasonings, for sufficiently small
\lambda>0
the interval
(-3\lambda,3\lambda)
contains no singularities of
\gamma(t)
. In particular,
\gamma(t)
is real analytic in
{\mathtt{L}}_{\lambda}:=(\lambda,2\lambda)
, with complex analyticity width
\lambda
, and the same holds also for
{||\left.\pi_{\Gamma^{m}}\nabla{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)\right|_{\gamma(t)}||_{2}}^{2}
in that interval. Hence, if the function
{||\left.\pi_{\Gamma^{m}}\nabla{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)\right|_{\gamma(t)}||_{2}}^{2}
has a zero of infinite order at some point
t^{\star}\in{\mathtt{L}}_{\lambda}
, then it is identically null in
{\mathtt{L}}_{\lambda}
.
Then, by Definition
1.1
, and by the minimality of
\gamma
, this implies that the polynomial
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)
cannot satisfy the steepness property at the origin on the subspace
\Gamma^{m}
.

We claim that a kind of converse result - involving
{\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda}
instead of
{\mathtt{L}}_{\lambda}
- is also true: if
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)
is non-steep at the origin on the subspace
\Gamma^{m}
, then
{||\left.\pi_{\Gamma^{m}}\nabla{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)\right|_{\gamma(t)}||_{2}}^{2}
must have a zero of infinite order in
{\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda}
. This observation is fundamental in order to prove Theorem A. Actually, the necessity of a zero of infinite order has been proved for a real-analytic function in
[
51
]
via the curve-selection Lemma; however, in the polynomial setting considered here, we have a much stronger quantitative result.

Motivated by this heuristic argument, we are interested in studying the properties of those real polynomials of
m\geq 1
variables whose gradient has a zero of sufficiently high order on some curve
\gamma
parametrized by one coordinate. In a first moment, we do not consider the fact that these polynomials are the restrictions to a
m
-dimensional subspace
\Gamma^{m}
of polynomials defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with
n>m
. This will be taken into account in section
6
. Therefore, we give the following definitions:

Definition 5.1
.

We indicate by
\Theta_{m}
the set of curves
\gamma(t)
with values in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
\gamma(t)
is real-analytic around the origin, and
\gamma(0)=\dot{\gamma}(0)=0
.

Remark 5.1
.

By the inverse function theorem, for any element
\gamma\in\Theta_{m}
there exists some neighborhood
U_{\gamma}
and some
k\in\{1,...,m\}
such that for all
t\in U_{\gamma}
one has the parametrization

\gamma(t):=\begin{cases}x_{k}(t)=t\\
x_{j}(t)=f_{j}(t)\quad\forall j\in\{1,...,m\}\ ,\ \ j\neq k\ .\end{cases}

(5.1)

From now on, when considering an element of
\Theta_{m}
, we will always assume implicitly that it is parametrized as in (
5.1
).

For fixed
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, we denote by
\Theta_{m}^{i}
the subset of curves in
\Theta_{m}
that can be parametrized by the
i
-th coordinate. Clearly, one has the decomposition

\Theta_{m}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\Theta_{m}^{i}\ .

Remark 5.2
.

We are asking the arc
\gamma
to be analytic at the origin, but the minimal arc obtained in Theorem
4.1
did not necessarily have this property (the origin was not included, in general, in the uniform interval of analyticity
{\mathtt{I}}_{\lambda}\subset[-\lambda,\lambda]
). As it has already been discussed in the introduction, this is an issue that comes from the use of analytic reparametrizations of semi-algebraic sets. We will deal with this apparent difficulty in section
6
.

Remark 5.3
.

For the moment, we do not make any assumption on the sizes of the neighborhoods of analyticity
U_{\gamma}
of the arcs in
\Theta_{m}
. Hence, the results of this section do not require any uniform lower bound on
|U_{\gamma}|
as in Theorem
4.1
. Nevertheless, the existence of a uniform lower bound will prove to be necessary in order to to demonstrate the results of section
6
.

Definition 5.2
.

For any pair of integers
s\geq 1
and
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, we indicate by

\vartheta_{m}(s)
(resp.
\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)
)
the subset of
({\mathcal{P}}^{\star}(s,1))^{m}={\mathcal{P}}^{\star}(s,1)\times\dots\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\star}(s,1)
containing the truncations at order
s
of the Taylor expansions at the origin of all curves in
\Theta_{m}
(resp. in
\Theta_{m}^{i}
). The elements of
\vartheta_{m}(s)
will henceforth be referred to as
s
-truncations.

Clearly, one has the following decomposition:

\vartheta_{m}(s)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)\ .

(5.2)

Remark 5.4
.

Clearly,
\vartheta_{m}(s)
(resp.
\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)
) is isomorphic to the set of
s
-jets of curves in
\Theta_{m}
(resp.
\Theta_{m}^{i}
). Moreover, for each
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, the set
\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)\times s}
, since for any curve
\gamma\in\Theta_{m}^{i}
its
s
-truncation
{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}\in\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)
is determined by the first
s
Taylor coefficients at the origin of the functions
f_{j}
, with
j\in\{1,...,m\}
,
j\neq i
.

Definition 5.3
.

Fix three integers
r\geq 2
,
m\geq 1
, and
1\leq s\leq r-1
. A polynomial
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
is said to be
s
-vanishing
if there exists an arc
\gamma\in\Theta_{m}
such that on its
s
-truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta_{m}(s)

the gradient of
P
has a zero of order
s
at the origin, namely

\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial P}{\partial x_{\ell}}\right|_{{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}(t)}\right)_{t=0}=0\ ,\lx@nobreakspace\lx@nobreakspace\forall\ \ell\in\{1,...,m\}\ ,\ \forall\ \alpha\in\{0,...,s\}\ .

(5.3)

The set of
s
-vanishing polynomials in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
is denoted by
\sigma(r,s,m)
.

In paragraph
5.2
, we shall investigate the properties of the set
\sigma(r,s,m)\subset\mathcal{P}(r,m)
of
s
-vanishing polynomials: in particular, we shall prove that

1.

for any given values of
r\geq 2
,
m\geq 1
,
1\leq s\leq r-1
, it is the semi-algebraic projection onto
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
of an algebraic set
Z(r,s,m)
of
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta_{m}(s)
whose ideal can be explicitly computed ;

2.

it has positive codimension.

Secondly, in paragraph
5.3
, we shall show that any polynomial
P
belonging to the complementary of the closure of
\sigma(r,s,m)
in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
satisfies a "stable" lower estimate on its gradient. As we shall see, "stable" means that the estimate holds uniformly true for any polynomial belonging to a neighborhood of
P
.

Finally, in section
6
, we shall prove that a polynomial
Q\in\mathcal{P}(r,n)
satisfying
\nabla Q(0)\neq 0
is steep around the origin iff there exists
1\leq s\leq r-1
such that, for all
m\in\{1,...,n-1\}
, the restriction of
Q
to any
m
-dimensional linear subspace
\Gamma^{m}
perpendicular to
\nabla Q(0)
is contained in the complementary of
\text{closure}(\sigma(r,s,m))
in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
.

5.2
Algebraic properties

We assume the notations of the previous paragraph, and we consider a triplet of integers
r\geq 2
,
m\geq 1
,
1\leq s\leq r-1
. We work in the euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{m}
equipped with coordinates
(x_{1},...,x_{m})
, and we consider a polynomial
P=P(x)\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
verifying the
s
-vanishing condition on the
s
-truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta_{m}(s)
of some curve
\gamma\in\Theta_{m}
.
Unless explicitly specified, we will henceforth work in the case in which
\gamma
is parametrized by the first coordinate, as the generalization to other cases is immediate. Hence, from now on we set
\gamma(t):=(t,x_{2}(t),\dots,x_{m}(t))\in\Theta_{m}^{1}
.

5.2.1
Case
m=1

We observe that, for
m=1
, we have the following simple result:

Lemma 5.1
.

For
m=1
, a polynomial
P(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}\\
1\leq\mu\leq r\end{subarray}}p_{\mu}x^{\mu}
of one real variable belongs to the set
\sigma(r,s,1)\subset{\mathcal{P}}(r,1)
if and only if

p_{\mu}=0\qquad\forall\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{ such that }1\leq\mu\leq s+1\ .

(5.4)

Moreover,
\sigma(r,s,1)
is closed and its codimension in
{\mathcal{P}}(r,1)
is equal to
s+1
.

Proof.

For
m=1
, the set
\Theta_{1}
is the singleton containing the line
\gamma(t):=x(t)=t
.

By Definition
5.3
, it is clear that a polynomial verifying condition (
5.4
) in the statement satisfies also the
s
-vanishing condition. Conversely, again by Definition
5.3
, it is plain to check that the
s
-vanishing condition for
m=1
imposes that the coefficients of the studied polynomial must be null up to order
s+1
. The closure of
\sigma(r,s,1)
is due to continuity, whereas
\text{codim }\sigma(r,s,1)=s+1
as such a set is determined by
s+1
independent equations in
{\mathcal{P}}(r,1)
.
∎

5.2.2
Notations (case
n\geq 3
,
2\leq m\leq n-1
)

Up to the end of this paragraph, we will restrict to the case
n\geq 3
,
2\leq m\leq n-1
. The goal is to introduce useful notations in order to study the properties of
s
-vanishing polynomials in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
.

Using standard notations, we set
\mu:=(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\ ,\ \ |\mu|:=||\mu||_{1}
, and for any
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
we write

P(x):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
1\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}p_{(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{m})}x_{1}^{\mu_{1}}...\,x_{m}^{\mu_{m}}=:\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
1\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}p_{\mu}x^{\mu}\ ,

(5.5)

where we have taken into account the fact that
P
has no constant term by the definition of
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
.

We also consider a curve
\gamma\in\Theta_{m}^{1}
and - for
j=2,...,m
- we develop its components
f_{j}
at the origin, and we write

x_{j}(t)=f_{j}(t)=:\sum_{i=1}^{+\infty}a_{ji}t^{i}\ ,

(5.6)

where we have taken into account the fact that
\gamma(0)=0
by Definition
5.1
. Thus, the
s
-truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
of the curve
\gamma
is identified by the
(m-1)s
real coefficients
(a_{21},...,a_{2s},...,a_{m1},...,a_{ms})
of the truncated expansion, namely

{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}={{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}(t)=\left(t,\sum_{i=1}^{s}a_{2i}t^{i}\,,\dots,\sum_{i=1}^{s}a_{mi}t^{i}\right)\ .

(5.7)

In the rest of this paragraph, we will try to find an explicit expression for the
s
-vanishing condition in terms of the coefficients of
P
and
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}
.
We first observe that the
s
-vanishing condition (
5.3
) for
\alpha=0
implies

p_{\mu}=0\quad\text{ for all }\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\text{ such that }|\mu|=1\ .

(5.8)

Thus, without any loss of generality, in (
5.5
) we can only consider the multi-indices
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
that satisfy
2\leq|\mu|\leq r
. Moreover, for
\ell=1,...,m
, the
\ell
-th component of the gradient of
P
reads

\frac{\partial P}{\partial x_{\ell}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
2\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ p_{\mu}x^{\widetilde{\mu}(\ell)}\ ,\ \ \widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell):=\mu_{j}-\delta_{j\ell}\ ,\ \ j=1,...,m\ ,\ \ |\mu|=|\widetilde{\mu}(\ell)|+1\ ,

(5.9)

where
\delta_{j\ell}
is the Kronecker symbol. At this point, we indicate by

\Phi^{1}:\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)s}\ ,\ \ M:=\dim\mathcal{P}(r,m)

(5.10)

the trivial chart associating

(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})\longmapsto(p_{\mu},a_{21},...,a_{2s},...,a_{m1},...,a_{ms})

and we define the functions

q_{\ell\alpha}^{1}:\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)s}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\ ,\ \ \ell\in\{1,...,m\}\ ,\ \ \alpha\in\{0,...,s\}

in the following way:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{ For }\alpha=0\qquad q_{\ell 0}^{1}\circ\Phi^{1}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}):=\left(\left.\frac{\partial P}{\partial x_{\ell}}\right|_{{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}(t)}\right)_{t=0}=\ p_{(0,...,0,1,0,...,0)}\\
&\text{ For }\alpha\in\{1,...,s\}\qquad q_{\ell\alpha}^{1}\circ\Phi^{1}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}):=\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial P}{\partial x_{\ell}}\right|_{{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}(t)}\right)_{t=0}\\
&=\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
2\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ p_{\mu}t^{\widetilde{\mu}_{1}(\ell)}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{s}a_{2k}t^{k}\right)^{\widetilde{\mu}_{2}(\ell)}...\left(\sum_{j=1}^{s}a_{mj}t^{j}\right)^{\widetilde{\mu}_{m}(\ell)}\right]_{t=0}\end{split}

(5.11)

where the "
1
" fills the
\ell
-th slot in the multi-index at the rightest member of the first line and where the last line is obtained by injecting (
5.7
) into expression (
5.9
).

Remark 5.5
.

In a similar way, when
\gamma
is parametrized by the
i
-th coordinate, with
i\neq 1
, one can denote by
\Phi^{i}:\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)s}

the chart associating

(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})\mapsto(p_{\mu},a_{11},...,a_{1s},...,a_{(i-1)1},...,a_{(i-1)s},a_{(i+1)1},...,a_{(i+1)s},...,a_{m1},...,a_{ms})
, and introduce the maps
q_{\ell\alpha}^{i}\circ\Phi^{i}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})
,
\ell\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}
exchanging the rôle of the first coordinate with that of the
i
-th coordinate in (
5.11
).

Remark 5.6
.

Comparing expressions (
5.11
) with Definition
5.3
and expression (
5.8
), and taking Remark
5.5
into account, it is easy to see that any given polynomial
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
satisfies the
s
-vanishing condition on some truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta_{m}(s)
if and only if

q_{\ell\alpha}^{i}\circ\Phi^{i}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})=0\ ,\ \ \text{ for some }i\in\{2,\dots,m\}\ ,\ \ \text{for all }\ell\in\{1,...,m\}\ ,\ \ \alpha\in\{0,...,s\}\ .

(5.12)

By the above discussion, we see that the set of
s
-vanishing polynomials in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
is given by

\sigma(r,s,m)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\sigma^{i}(r,s,m)\ ,

(5.13)

where we have introduced the sets

\sigma^{i}(r,s,m):=\Pi_{\mathcal{P}(r,m)}Z^{i}(r,s,m)

(5.14)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z^{i}(r,s,m):=\{&(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)\}|\\
&q_{\ell\alpha}^{i}\circ\Phi^{i}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})=0\text{ for all }\ell\in\{1,...,m\},\alpha\in\{0,...,s\}\}\ .\end{split}

(5.15)

We also set

Z(r,s,m):=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}Z^{i}(r,s,m)\ .

(5.16)

It turns out that the ideal of
Z(r,s,m)
can be explicitly computed for any given value of the integers
r\geq 2
,
1\leq s\leq r-1
, and
m\geq 2

13
13

13

The case
m=1
is easier, see Lemma
5.1
.
, i.e., one can find explicit expressions for the quantities
q_{\ell\alpha}^{i}\circ\Phi^{i}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})
, for any value of
\ell\in\{1,...,m\}
,
\alpha\in\{0,...,s\}
, and
i\in\{1,...,m\}
.

Before stating this result, for any given value of
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
we will introduce new global charts for
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)
which - though unessential for the validity of our results - yield nicer expressions for the equations
q_{\ell\alpha}^{i}\circ\Phi^{i}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})=0
than the standard chart
\Phi^{i}
.
As it will be shown in the next paragraph, the variables
a_{j1},j\neq i,
associated to the linear terms of the
s
-truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}
can be incorporated in the coordinates of the polynomial
P
. This simplifies the calculations and yields more readable formulas.

5.2.3
A useful chart for
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta_{m}(s)
(case
n\geq 3
,
2\leq m\leq n-1
)

Here too, we restrict to the case
n\geq 3
,
2\leq m\leq n-1
.

Once again, we only consider the case in which
\gamma
is parametrized by the coordinate
i=1
, the other cases being trivial generalizations. Some of the quantities introduced in the sequel should be labeled with an index
1
, as their definition depends in an obvious way from the choice of the parametrizing coordinate. However, in order not to burden notations, we drop, when possible, the reference to the fact that we are considering the case
i=1
.

In order to define a new chart for
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
, we start by observing that, if we denote by
A_{1},...,A_{m}
the canonical basis associated to the coordinates
x_{1},...,x_{m}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, for any fixed vector
{\mathtt{b}}:=(b_{21},...,b_{m1})\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
, we can define the new parametric basis

v_{\mathtt{b}}:=A_{1}+b_{21}A_{2}+b_{31}A_{3}+...+b_{m1}A_{m}\ ,\ \ u_{2}:=A_{2}\ ,\ \ ...\ ,\ \ u_{m}:=A_{m}

(5.17)

associated to the parametric change of variables

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{b}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}\ ,\ \ \lx@nobreakspace(x_{1},...,x_{m})\longmapsto{\mathtt{y}}({\mathtt{b}}):=({\mathtt{y}}_{1},{\mathtt{y}}_{2}({\mathtt{b}}),...,{\mathtt{y}}_{m}({\mathtt{b}})),

(5.18)

where

{\mathtt{y}}_{1}:=x_{1}\quad,\qquad{\mathtt{y}}_{2}={\mathtt{y}}_{2}({\mathtt{b}}):=x_{2}-b_{21}\,x_{1}\quad\dots\quad{\mathtt{y}}_{m}={\mathtt{y}}_{m}({\mathtt{b}}):=x_{m}-b_{m1}\,x_{1}\ .

(5.19)

Obviously, for any fixed
{\mathtt{b}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
, the change of coordinates (
5.19
) in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
induces a change of coordinates also in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
. Infact, the pull-back of the polynomial
P(x)
is indicated by

{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{b}}({\mathtt{y}}({\mathtt{b}})):=P\circ{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{b}}^{-1}({\mathtt{y}}({\mathtt{b}}))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
2\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}p_{\mu}(x({\mathtt{y}}({\mathtt{b}})))^{\mu}=:\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
2\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}{\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}(p_{\mu},{\mathtt{b}}){\mathtt{y}}^{\mu}({\mathtt{b}})\ ,

(5.20)

where the new coefficients
{\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}={\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}(p_{\mu},b_{21},....,b_{m1})
are polynomial functions of coefficients
p_{\mu}
and on the parameters
{\mathtt{b}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
. For any given
{\mathtt{b}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
, there is a
1-1
correspondence between the quantities
p_{\mu}
and
{\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}
, as they represent the coordinates of the same polynomial written in different bases. Moreover, by (
5.6
), in the new variables (
5.19
) the components of the push-forward
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{b}}\circ{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}={\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{b}}\circ{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
of any
s
-truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
read

{\mathtt{y}}_{1}(t)=t\quad,\qquad{\mathtt{y}}_{j}(t):=(a_{j1}-b_{j1})t+\sum_{i=2}^{s}a_{ji}t^{i}\quad,\qquad j\in\{2,...,m\}\ .

(5.21)

By looking at expression (
5.21
), we see that, for any
s\geq 2
and for any given
s
-truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
, it is possible to find a parametric change of coordinates
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{b}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that the image
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{b}}\circ{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
has no linear terms except for the parametrizing component: it suffices to choose
{\mathtt{b}}={\mathtt{a}}:=(a_{21},...,a_{m1})
, with
(a_{21},...,a_{m1})
the coefficients of the linear terms of
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}
. Taking (
5.20
) into account, this also entails that, for any given truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
, there exists an associated set of coordinates
{\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}={\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}(p_{\mu},{\mathtt{a}})
, with
{\mathtt{a}}:=(a_{21},...,a_{m1})
, in the space of polynomials
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
. Hence, we can parametrize the coordinates of any polynomial
P
through the linear coefficients of any truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}
.

Namely, we firstly observe that
\vartheta^{1}_{m}(1)
is isomorphic to

\vartheta^{1}_{m}(1)\simeq\{{\mathtt{a}}:=(a_{21},\dots,a_{m1})\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1}\}\ .

(5.22)

Also, indicating by
\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
the subset of
s
-truncations having null linear terms, we have that

\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)\simeq\{(a_{22},\dots,a_{2s},\dots,a_{m2},\dots,a_{ms})\in\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)(s-1)}\}\ .

(5.23)

Clearly, one has

\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)=\vartheta^{1}_{m}(1)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)\ .

(5.24)

At this point, with the notation in (
5.20
), we define the invertible transformation

\mathscr{F}^{1}:\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1}\quad,\qquad M:=\dim\mathcal{P}(r,m)

(5.25)

associating

\left(P=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
1\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}p_{\mu}x^{\mu},{\mathtt{a}}\right)\longmapsto\left({\mathtt{p}}_{\mu^{\prime}}\left((p_{\mu})_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
1\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}},{\mathtt{a}}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
1\leq|\mu^{\prime}|\leq r\end{subarray}},{\mathtt{a}}\right)

(5.26)

and we indicate its image by

W^{1}(r,m):=\mathscr{F}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1})\ .

(5.27)

In other words,
W^{1}(r,m)
is constructed by attaching to any point
{\mathtt{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m-1}\simeq\vartheta^{1}_{m}(1)
the fiber of all polynomials in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
expressed in the variables (
5.19
) associated to the value
{\mathtt{a}}
.

Furthermore, setting

{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}:={\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}

(5.28)

we have
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
by construction because in the adapted variables - as we had shown in
(
5.21
)
by setting
{\mathtt{b}}={\mathtt{a}}
- with the exception of the parametrizing component, any truncation starts at order two. Taking the notation in (
5.20
) into account, we can also define the chart

\Upsilon^{1}:\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)\longrightarrow W^{1}(r,m)\times\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)(s-1)}

(5.29)

associating

(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})\longmapsto\left({\mathtt{p}}_{\mu^{\prime}}\left((p_{\mu})_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
1\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}},{\mathtt{a}}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
1\leq|\mu^{\prime}|\leq r\end{subarray}},\ {\mathtt{a}},a_{22},a_{23},...,a_{ms}\right)\ .

(5.30)

Remark 5.7
.

For further convenience, we also denote by

{\mathfrak{U}}^{1}:\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(1)\longrightarrow W^{1}(r,m)

(5.31)

the restriction of
\Upsilon^{1}
to
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(1)
. It is plain to check by formulas (
5.20
), (
5.26
), and (
5.30
) that
{\mathfrak{U}}^{1}
is polynomial, invertible and that its inverse is a polynomial map.

Remark 5.8
.

The generalization of the arguments above to the case in which the curve
\gamma\in\Theta_{m}
is parametrized by the
i
-th coordinate, with
i\in\{2,\dots,m\}
, is immediate. In particular, one can define functions
\mathscr{F}^{i}
,
\Upsilon^{i}
,
{\mathfrak{U}}^{i}
, together with sets
W^{i}(r,m)
.

Remark 5.9
.

With slight abuse of notation, in the rest of this work we will often write
({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})
and
({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}})
to indicate a point of
\Upsilon^{1}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})
and
W^{1}(r,m)
respectively.

For further convenience, we also observe that

Lemma 5.2
.

Any polynomial
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
satisfies the
s
-vanishing condition

\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial P(x)}{\partial x_{\ell}}\right|_{{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}(t)}\right)_{t=0}=0\quad\forall\alpha\in\{0,...,s\}\ ,\ \ \forall\ell\in\{1,...,m\}

(5.32)

on the
s
-truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
of some curve
\gamma\in\Theta_{m}^{1}
, if and only if it satisfies

\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})}{\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)}\right)_{t=0}=0\quad\forall\alpha\in\{0,...,s\}\ ,\ \ \forall\ell\in\{1,...,m\}

in the adapted coordinates associated to the linear terms of
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}
.

Proof.

By (
5.19
) one has

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{-1}({\mathtt{y}}):=\begin{cases}x_{1}={\mathtt{y}}_{1}\\
x_{2}=a_{21}\,{\mathtt{y}}_{1}+{\mathtt{y}}_{2}\\
...\\
x_{m}=a_{m1}\,{\mathtt{y}}_{1}+{\mathtt{y}}_{m}\\
\end{cases}\ ,\ \ {\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{-1}({\mathtt{y}})=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0&0&...&0\\
a_{21}&1&0&...&0\\
a_{31}&0&1&...&0\\
...&...&...&...&...\\
a_{m1}&0&0&...&1\\
\end{matrix}\right)\ .

(5.33)

Indicating with
({\mathcal{L}}^{-1}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}}))_{k,\ell}
the
(k,\ell)
-th entry of the Jacobian of the inverse transformation
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{-1}
in (
5.33
), by the Leibniz formula one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})}{\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)}\right)_{t=0}:=\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial(P\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}}))}{\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)}\right)_{t=0}\\
=&\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left.\frac{\partial P}{\partial x_{k}}\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})\right|_{{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}(t)}\times\left.({\mathcal{L}}^{-1}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}}))_{k,\ell}\right|_{{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)}\right]_{t=0}\\
=&\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left.\frac{\partial P(x)}{\partial x_{k}}\right|_{{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}(t)}\times\left.({\mathcal{L}}^{-1}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}}))_{k,\ell}\right|_{{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)}\right]_{t=0}\ .\end{split}

(5.34)

Since the entries of the matrix
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{-1}
in (
5.33
) are constant, one has

\left.({\mathcal{L}}^{-1}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}}))_{k,\ell}\right|_{{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)}=({\mathcal{L}}^{-1}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}}))_{k,\ell}\ ,

so that, finally, (
5.34
) reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})}{\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)}\right)_{t=0}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left[\left.\frac{\partial P(x)}{\partial x_{k}}\right|_{{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}(t)}\right]_{t=0}\times({\mathcal{L}}^{-1}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}}))_{k,\ell}\ .\end{split}

(5.35)

By the expression above, it is immediate to check that if
P
satisfies the
s
-vanishing condition on
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}
then
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
does the same on
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}
.
The proof of the converse is immediate by applying the same arguments to
P(x)\equiv{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}(x)
.
∎

By the discussion above, one has the choice to write the equations determining the set
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
in (
5.15
) either in the original coordinates, where they assume the form
q^{1}_{\ell\alpha}\circ\Phi(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})=0
for all
\ell\in\{1...,m\}
and
\alpha\in\{0,...,s\}
, or in the new set of coordinates associated to the change of variables
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, defined in (
5.19
). In particular, by performing the same computations that led to expression (
5.11
) in the new variables, and by taking into account the fact that the expansion of
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)
starts at order two in
t
, one can introduce the functions

{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}:W^{1}(r,m)\times\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)(s-1)}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\quad,\qquad\ell\in\{1,\dots,m\}\quad\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}

(5.36)

in the following way:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{For }\alpha=0,\qquad{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell 0}\circ\Upsilon^{1}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})={\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell 0}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}):=\ {\mathtt{p}}_{(0,\dots,0,1,0,\dots,0)}\\
&\text{For }\alpha\in\{1,...,s\},\ \ \ {\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}\circ\Upsilon^{1}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})={\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}):=\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})}{\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)}\right)_{t=0}\\
&=\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
2\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}t^{\widetilde{\mu}_{1}(\ell)}\left(\sum_{k=2}^{s}a_{2k}t^{k}\right)^{\widetilde{\mu}_{2}(\ell)}\dots\left(\sum_{j=2}^{s}a_{mj}t^{j}\right)^{\widetilde{\mu}_{m}(\ell)}\right]_{t=0}\ .\end{split}

(5.37)

Expressions (
5.11
) and (
5.37
), considered together with Lemma
5.2
, imply that condition
q_{\ell\alpha}^{1}\circ\Phi^{1}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})=0
holds if and only if

{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}\circ\Upsilon^{1}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})={\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0\ ,\ \ \lx@nobreakspace\forall\ell\in\{1,\dots,m\},\forall\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}\ ,

(5.38)

so that the ideal of the set
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
in the new variables is given by the equations
{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}\circ\Upsilon^{1}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})={\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
for all
\ell\in\{1...,m\}
and
\alpha\in\{0,...,s\}
.
In the sequel, we will work in these new coordinates, since the involved expressions are nicer.

5.2.4
Computations and estimate on the codimension of
\sigma(r,s,m)
(case
n\geq 3
,
2\leq m\leq n-1
)

As in the previous paragraphs, we set
n\geq 3
,
2\leq m\leq n-1
.

Once again, we will only consider the case in which
\gamma
is parametrized by the first coordinate.

Before stating the main results of this paragraph, we still need to introduce a few notations. For any fixed
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}
, for any
\beta\in\{0,\dots,\alpha\}
, and for any
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, we set

\displaystyle\nu(i,\beta):=\begin{cases}(\beta+1,0,\dots,0)\ ,\ &\text{ for }i=1\\
(\beta,0,\dots,0,1,0,\dots,0)\ ,\ &\text{ for }i=2,\dots,m\\
\end{cases}

(5.39)

where the "
1
" fills the
i
-th slot for
i=2,...,m
. For
\alpha\in\{1,...,s\}
, we also denote the multi-indices
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
of length
2\leq|\mu|\leq\alpha+1
not belonging to this family with

\mathcal{M}_{m}(\alpha):=\{\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\ ,\ \ 2\leq|\mu|\leq\alpha+1\}\backslash\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1,...,m\\
\beta=1,...,\alpha\end{subarray}}\{\nu(i,\beta)\}\ .

(5.40)

Moreover, for any given
\alpha\in\{1,..,s\}
,
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
and
\ell\in\{1,...,m\}
we introduce

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}(\ell),\alpha):=\biggl\{&(k_{j2},...,k_{j\alpha})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{(m-1)\times(\alpha-1)},j\in\{2,...,m\}:\\
&\sum_{i=2}^{\alpha}k_{ji}=\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)\ ,\ \ \widetilde{\mu}_{1}(\ell)+\sum_{j=2}^{m}\sum_{i=2}^{\alpha}i\ k_{ji}=\alpha\biggl\}\end{split}

(5.41)

and we set

{\mathcal{E}}_{m}(\ell,\alpha):=\{\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\ |\ \mathcal{G}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}(\ell),\alpha)\neq\varnothing\}\ .

(5.42)

Finally, for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and for any
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
, we remind that (see (
5.9
))

\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell):=\mu_{j}-\delta_{j\ell}\quad j\in\{1,\dots,m\}\ .

With these notations, we can now state the following

Theorem 5.1
.

For any choice of integers
m\geq 1
,
r\geq 2
,
1\leq s\leq r-1
, the set
Z(r,s,m)
in (
5.16
) is an algebraic set of
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta_{m}(s)
, whose ideal can be explicitly computed. In particular, with the notations in (
5.37
), (
5.39
), (
5.40
) and (
5.41
), the set
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
in (
5.15
) is the image through the inverse of the transformation
\Upsilon^{1}
in (
5.30
) of the algebraic set determined by the following equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathtt{Q}}_{10}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})&={\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(1,0)}=0\ ,\\
{\mathtt{Q}}_{11}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})&=2\,{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(1,1)}=0\ ,\\
\frac{{\mathtt{Q}}_{1\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})}{\alpha!}&=(\alpha+1){\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(1,\alpha)}+\sum_{i=2}^{m}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\alpha-1}\beta\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(i,\beta)}a_{i(\alpha-(\beta-1))}\\
+&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathcal{E}}_{m}(1,\alpha)\\
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{m}(\alpha)\\
\mu_{1}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mu_{1}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}(1),\alpha)}\left(\prod_{j=2}^{m}\binom{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(1)}{k_{j2}\ ...\ k_{j\alpha}}\ a_{j2}^{k_{j2}}...a_{j\alpha}^{k_{j\alpha}}\right)=0\ ,\\
\end{split}

(5.43)

for
\ell=1
,
\alpha=2,...,s
, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell 0}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})&={\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(\ell,0)}=0\ ,\\
{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell 1}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})&={\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(\ell,1)}=0\ ,\\
\frac{{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})}{\alpha!}&={\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(\ell,\alpha)}\\
+&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathcal{E}}_{m}(\ell,\alpha)\\
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{m}(\alpha)\\
\mu_{\ell}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}(\ell),\alpha)}\left(\prod_{j=2}^{m}\binom{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)}{k_{j2}\ ...\ k_{j\alpha}}\ a_{j2}^{k_{j2}}...a_{j\alpha}^{k_{j\alpha}}\right)=0\ ,\\
\text{for }\ell=2,...,m\ ,\ \ &\alpha=2,...,s\ .\end{split}

(5.44)

Remark 5.10
.

It is plain to check that the coefficients of the vector
{\mathtt{a}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(1)
containing the linear terms of the truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}
do not appear explicitly in expressions (
5.43
)-(
5.44
). However, they are "hidden" in the terms
{\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}={\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}(p_{\mu},{\mathtt{a}})
(see (
5.20
)).

As an almost immediate consequence of Theorem
5.1
, we have that
s
-vanishing polynomials are rare in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, namely

Corollary 5.1
.

Z(r,s,m)
has codimension
m(s+1)
in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta_{m}(s)
and
\sigma(r,s,m)
is a semi-algebraic set of codimension
s+m
in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
.

Proof.

(Theorem
5.1
)

For fixed
s\in\{1,...,r-1\}
, we consider a polynomial
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
verifying the
s
-vanishing condition on some truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}(t)\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
.

Step 1.

By Lemma
5.2
, in the adapted coordinates (
5.19
) one must have

\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})}{\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)}\right)_{t=0}=0\qquad\forall\alpha\in\{0,...,s\}\ ,\ \ \forall\ell\in\{1,...,m\}\ .

(5.45)

For
\alpha=0
, it is plain to check that the terms of order zero in
t
in (
5.45
) are the linear terms of
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
, for which
|\mu|=1
. Expressions (
5.45
) and (
5.37
) yield the thesis for this value of
\alpha
.

Then, as we did in (
5.9
), we drop the linear terms in
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
and we write the quantity
\partial{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})/\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}
explicitly

\frac{\partial{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})}{\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
2\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}{\mathtt{y}}^{\widetilde{\mu}(\ell)}\ ,\ \ \widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell):=\mu_{j}-\delta_{j\ell}\ ,\ \ j=1,...,m\ ,\ \ |\mu|=|\widetilde{\mu}(\ell)|+1\ .

(5.46)

Taking (
5.21
) into account (with
{\mathtt{b}}:=(b_{21},\dots,b_{m1})={\mathtt{a}}:=(a_{21},\dots,a_{m1})
), we inject in (
5.46
) the components of the
s
-truncation
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)
, namely

{\mathtt{y}}_{1}(t)=t\quad,\qquad{\mathtt{y}}_{j}(t)=\sum_{i=2}^{s}a_{ji}t^{i}\quad,\qquad j\in\{2,...,m\}\ ,

(5.47)

and we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.\frac{\partial{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})}{\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}(t)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
2\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}t^{\widetilde{\mu}_{1}(\ell)}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{s}a_{2i}t^{i}\right)^{\widetilde{\mu}_{2}(\ell)}...\left(\sum_{u=2}^{s}a_{mu}t^{u}\right)^{\widetilde{\mu}_{m}(\ell)}\ .\end{split}

(5.48)

Step 2.
For
\alpha=1
, we must look for the coefficient of the linear term (in
t
) in expression (
5.48
). Hence, for fixed
\ell=1,...,m
, since the sums in (
5.48
) start at order two in
t
, only the multi-index for which
\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)=0
for all
j\in\{2,...,m\}
and
\widetilde{\mu}_{1}(\ell)=1
must be retained in the sum in expression (
5.48
). The first condition implies
\mu_{j}(\ell)=\delta_{j\ell}
for all
j\in\{2,...,m\}
, whereas the second yields
\mu_{1}(\ell)=1+\delta_{1\ell}
. Therefore, by definition (
5.39
), for fixed
\ell\in\{1,...,m\}
only the multi-index
\nu(\ell,1)
appears in expression (
5.48
) for
\alpha=1
. Again by (
5.39
), one has
\nu_{1}(1,1)=2
and
\nu_{\ell}(\ell,1)=1
for
\ell\in\{2,...,m\}
so that the thesis in the case
\alpha=1
follows.

Step 3.
For any given
\alpha\in\{2,...,s\}
, we are interested in the coefficients of the terms of order
t^{\alpha}
in (
5.48
). Hence, we can truncate the internal sums in (
5.48
) at order
\alpha
. For the same reason, for any
\ell\in\{1,...,m\}
, we can neglect from the leftmost sum in (
5.48
) the monomials
\mu
satisfying
|\widetilde{\mu}(\ell)|>\alpha
(hence
|\mu|>\alpha+1
), as their contribution is of order at least
t^{\alpha+1}
. Thus, for fixed
\alpha\in\{2,...,s\}
and
\ell\in\{1,...,m\}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})\\
&=\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
2\leq|\mu|\leq\alpha+1\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}{\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}t^{\widetilde{\mu}_{1}(\ell)}\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\alpha}a_{2i}t^{i}\right)^{\widetilde{\mu}_{2}(\ell)}...\left(\sum_{u=2}^{\alpha}a_{mu}t^{u}\right)^{\widetilde{\mu}_{m}(\ell)}\right)_{t=0}\end{split}

(5.49)

due to formula (
5.37
).
Now, for any
j=2,\dots,m
and
\ell\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, the multinomial expansion yields:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left(\sum_{i=2}^{\alpha}a_{ji}t^{i}\right)^{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{j2},...,k_{j\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}\\
k_{j2}+...+k_{j\alpha}=\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)\end{subarray}}\binom{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)}{k_{j2}\ ...\ k_{j\alpha}}\ a_{j2}^{k_{j2}}\ ...\ a_{j\alpha}^{k_{j\alpha}}\ \ t^{2k_{j2}+....+\alpha k_{j\alpha}}\end{split}\ ,

(5.50)

where we have used the notation
\displaystyle\binom{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)}{k_{j2}\ ...\ k_{j\alpha}}:=\frac{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)!}{k_{j2}!\ ...\ k_{j\alpha}!}
.

Replacing each truncated Taylor development in (
5.49
) by its multinomial expansion (
5.50
), expression (
5.49
) reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}&\left[\rule{0.0pt}{31.2982pt}\right.\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
2\leq|\mu|\leq\alpha+1\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\ t^{\widetilde{\mu}_{1}(\ell)}\prod_{j=2}^{m}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{j2},...,k_{j\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}\\
k_{j2}+...+k_{j\alpha}=\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)\end{subarray}}\binom{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)}{k_{j2}\ ...\ k_{j\alpha}}\ a_{j2}^{k_{j2}}...a_{j\alpha}^{k_{j\alpha}}\ \ t^{2k_{j2}+....+\alpha k_{j\alpha}}\right)\left.\rule{0.0pt}{31.2982pt}\right]_{t=0}=\\
\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}&\left[\rule{0.0pt}{31.2982pt}\right.\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
2\leq|\mu|\leq\alpha+1\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\ t^{\widetilde{\mu}_{1}(\ell)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{N}^{(m-1)\times(\alpha-1)}\\
k=(k_{22},\dots,k_{2\alpha},\dots,k_{m2},\dots,k_{m\alpha})\\
\forall i\in\{2,\dots,m\}\\
k_{i2}+...+k_{i\alpha}=\widetilde{\mu}_{i}(\ell)\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=2}^{m}\binom{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)}{k_{j2}\ ...k_{j\alpha}}a_{j2}^{k_{j2}}...a_{j\alpha}^{k_{j\alpha}}\ \ t^{2k_{j2}+....+\alpha k_{j\alpha}}\left.\rule{0.0pt}{31.2982pt}\right]_{t=0}.\end{split}

(5.51)

Moreover, taking (
5.41
) into account, the class of multi-indices
{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}(\ell),\alpha)
selects those terms whose contribution inside the brackets of (
5.51
) is of order
t^{\alpha}
. Hence, by the above discussion, by (
5.37
) and by (
5.42
), for any fixed
\alpha\in\{2,...,s\}
, and
\ell\in\{1,...,m\}
, we can write

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=&\alpha!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathcal{E}}_{m}(\ell,\alpha)\\
\mu_{\ell}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}(\ell),\alpha)}\left(\prod_{j=2}^{m}\binom{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)}{k_{j2}\ ...\ k_{j\alpha}}\ a_{j2}^{k_{j2}}...a_{j\alpha}^{k_{j\alpha}}\right)\,.\end{split}

(5.52)

Now, we split the leftmost sum in (
5.52
) into the partial sums with respect to the families of indices defined in (
5.39
) and (
5.40
), namely for any fixed
\alpha\in\{2,...,s\}
and
\ell\in\{1,...,m\}
, we write

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})}{\alpha!}\\
=&\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu_{\ell}(i,\beta)\in{\mathcal{E}}_{m}(\ell,\alpha)\\
\nu_{\ell}(i,\beta)\neq 0\end{subarray}}{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\alpha}}\nu_{\ell}(i,\beta)\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(i,\beta)}\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}(\ell),\alpha)}\left(\prod_{j=2}^{m}\binom{\widetilde{\nu}_{j}(i,\beta)(\ell)}{k_{j2}\ \dots\ k_{j\alpha}}\ a_{j2}^{k_{j2}}\dots a_{j\alpha}^{k_{j\alpha}}\right)\\
+&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathcal{E}}_{m}(\ell,\alpha)\\
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{m}(\alpha)\\
\mu_{\ell}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}(\ell),\alpha)}\left(\prod_{j=2}^{m}\binom{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)}{k_{j2}\ \dots\ k_{j\alpha}}\ a_{j2}^{k_{j2}}\dots a_{j\alpha}^{k_{j\alpha}}\right)\ .\end{split}

(5.53)

Step 4.
We first study the case in which
\ell\neq 1
. For fixed
\ell\in\{2,...,m\}
, for any
i\in\{1,...,m\}
,
i\neq\ell
, and for any
\beta\in\{1,...,\alpha\}
, the monomials corresponding to the indices
\nu(i,\beta)
do not contribute to the leftmost sum at the r.h.s. of (
5.53
). Infact, by (
5.39
), the
\ell
-th element
\nu_{\ell}(i,\beta)
of multi-index
\nu(i,\beta)
is equal to zero for
\ell\in\{2,...,m\}
and
i\in\{1,...,m\}
,
i\neq\ell
.

Moreover, still for fixed
\ell\in\{2,...,m\}
, the indices
\nu(\ell,\beta)
, with
\beta\in\{1,...,\alpha\}
, satisfy
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}(\ell,\beta)(\ell)=\beta
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{j}(\ell,\beta)(\ell)=0
for all
j\in\{2,...,m\}
, so that by (
5.41
) we have

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\widetilde{\nu}(\ell,\beta)(\ell),\alpha)=\begin{cases}\varnothing\ ,\ \ &\text{if }\beta=1,...,\alpha-1\\
\{0\}\ ,\ \ &\text{if }\beta=\alpha\\
\end{cases}\ .

Consequently, the only monomial that contributes to the leftmost sum at the r.h.s. of (
5.53
) is the one associated to the multi-index
\nu(\ell,\alpha)
, and one has
k_{j2}=0,\dots,k_{j\alpha}=0
when
\mu=\nu(\ell,\alpha)
.
Moreover, by hypothesis we have
\nu_{\ell}(\ell,\alpha)=1
for any
\ell\in\{2,...,m\}
.
Due to these arguments, for any fixed
\ell\in\{2,...,m\}
, we can rewrite (
5.53
) in the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})}{\alpha!}\\
&={\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(\ell,\alpha)}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathcal{E}}_{m}(\ell,\alpha)\\
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{m}(\alpha)\\
\mu_{\ell}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mu_{\ell}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}(\ell),\alpha)}\left(\prod_{j=2}^{m}\binom{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(\ell)}{k_{j2}\ ...\ k_{j\alpha}}\ a_{j2}^{k_{j2}}...a_{j\alpha}^{k_{j\alpha}}\right)\ .\end{split}

(5.54)

This proves the Lemma for
\ell=2,...,m
,
\alpha=2,...,s
.

Step 5.
We now consider the case
\ell=1
. For all
j\in\{2,...,m\}
, the sub-family of indices
\nu(1,\beta)
, with
\beta\in\{1,...,\alpha\}
, satisfies
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}(1,\beta)(1)=\beta
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{j}(1,\beta)(1)=0
. Hence, thanks to (
5.41
), we find

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\widetilde{\nu}(1,\beta)(1),\alpha)=\begin{cases}\varnothing\ ,\ \ &\text{if }\beta=1,...,\alpha-1\\
\{0\}\ ,\ \ &\text{if }\beta=\alpha\\
\end{cases}\ .

(5.55)

Moreover, we have
\nu_{1}(1,\alpha)=\alpha+1
by construction.

On the other hand, for
\ell=1
,
\beta\in\{1,...,\alpha\}
and
i,j\in\{2,...,m\}
, the multi-indices
\nu(i,\beta)
satisfy
\widetilde{\nu}_{1}(i,\beta)(1)=\beta-1
and
\widetilde{\nu}_{j}(i,\beta)(1)=\delta_{ji}
. Hence, by (
5.41
) one can write

\widetilde{\nu}_{j}(i,\beta)(1)=\delta_{ji}=\sum_{u=2}^{\alpha}k_{ju}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ k_{ju}=\delta_{ji}\delta_{uv}\text{ for some $v\in\{2,...,\alpha\}$}

(5.56)

and

\widetilde{\nu}_{1}(i,\beta)(1)+\sum_{j=2}^{m}\sum_{u=2}^{\alpha}u\ k_{ju}=\beta-1+\sum_{j=2}^{m}\sum_{u=2}^{\alpha}u\ k_{ju}=\alpha\ .

(5.57)

By (
5.56
), we see that condition (
5.57
) can be satisfied by some vector of multi-integers
(k_{22},\dots,k_{2\alpha},\dots,k_{m2},\dots,k_{m\alpha})
if
\beta\in\{1,...,\alpha-1\}
, but cannot be fulfilled for
\beta=\alpha
.
Injecting (
5.56
) into (
5.57
) one has

\sum_{j=2}^{m}\sum_{u=2}^{\alpha}\,u\,\delta_{ji}\delta_{uv}=\alpha-(\beta-1)\quad\text{ for all }\quad\beta\in\{1,...,\alpha-1\}\ ,\ \ i\in\{2,...,m\}

(5.58)

which implies

k_{ju}=\delta_{ji}\,\delta_{uv}\,\delta_{v,\alpha-(\beta-1)}\quad\text{ for all }\quad\beta\in\{1,...,\alpha-1\}\ ,\ \ i\in\{2,...,m\}\ .

(5.59)

Hence, for all
\beta\in\{1,...,\alpha-1\}
, and
i\in\{2,...,m\}
, we can finally write

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\widetilde{\nu}(i,\beta)(1),\alpha)=\{(k_{j1},...,k_{j\alpha}),\ j\in\{2,...,m\},\ k_{ju}=\delta_{ji}\delta_{u,\alpha-(\beta-1)}\}

(5.60)

and

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\widetilde{\nu}(i,\alpha)(1),\alpha)=\varnothing\ .

(5.61)

Moreover, for
i=2,...,m
, by (
5.39
) we have
\nu_{1}(i,\beta)=\beta
.

By taking (
5.55
), (
5.60
), (
5.61
) into account, expression (
5.53
) with
\ell=1
yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{{\mathtt{Q}}_{1\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})}{\alpha!}=(\alpha+1){\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(1,\alpha)}+\sum_{i=2}^{m}\sum_{\beta=1}^{\alpha-1}\beta\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(i,\beta)}a_{i(\alpha-(\beta-1))}\\
+&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathcal{E}}_{m}(1,\alpha)\\
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{m}(\alpha)\\
\mu_{1}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\mu_{1}\ {\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}\sum_{k\in{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}(1),\alpha)}\left(\prod_{j=2}^{m}\binom{\widetilde{\mu}_{j}(1)}{k_{j2}\ ...\ k_{j\alpha}}\ a_{j2}^{k_{j2}}...a_{j\alpha}^{k_{j\alpha}}\right)\ .\\
\end{split}

(5.62)

This concludes the proof for the case in which
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
.

The proof of the case in which
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)
, with
i=2,\dots,m
, is the same: one just has to take into account that the rôle of the special index is played by
i
instead of
1
. Hence, the ideals of the sets
Z^{i}(r,s,m)
can be explicitly computed and, by expression (
5.16
), the proof is concluded.

∎

We are now able to prove that
s
-vanishing polynomials are rare in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
.

Proof.

(Corollary
5.1
)

We want to show that, for a given pair
(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
, the
ms+m
equations in (
5.43
) and (
5.44
) are all linearly independent.

For all
\ell=1,...,m
and
\alpha=0,...,s
, we collect in table (
5.64
) the derivatives of the functions
{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}/\alpha!
defined in (
5.36
)-(
5.37
) - and whose action is made explicit in (
5.43
) - (
5.44
) - with respect to the coefficients of
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
,
{\mathtt{a}}
, and to the Taylor coefficients of
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}
. We have indicated

1.

with the symbol
\mathbb{D}
, the
s\times s
diagonal matrix whose entries are the numbers
\alpha+1
, for
\alpha=1,...,s
;

2.

with the symbol
\mathbb{I}_{s}
, the
s\times s
identity matrix;

3.

with the symbol
\mathbb{B}_{i}
,
i\in\{2,...,m\}
, an
s\times s
matrix whose entry at position
\alpha,\beta
, with
\alpha\in\{1,...,s\}
and
\beta\in\{1,...,s\}
, reads

(\mathbb{B}_{i})_{\alpha,\beta}:=\begin{cases}0\ ,\ \ &\text{if }\alpha=1\ ,\\
\beta a_{i(\alpha-(\beta-1))}\ ,\ \ &\text{if }2\leq\alpha\leq s\ ,\ \ 1\leq\beta\leq\alpha-1\ ,\\
0\ ,\ \ &\text{if }2\leq\alpha\leq s\ ,\ \ \alpha\leq\beta\leq s\ .\end{cases}

(5.63)

{\begin{matrix}\ell&\alpha&\partial_{a_{ji}}&\partial_{{\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}}&\partial_{{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(i,0)}}&\partial_{{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(1,\beta)}}&\partial_{{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(2,\beta)}}&\dots&\partial_{{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(m,\beta)}}\\
&&&\mu\in\mathcal{M}(s)&i=1,\dots,m&\beta=1,\dots,s&\beta=1,\dots,s&\dots&\beta=1,\dots,s\\
\\
1,\dots,m&0&0&0&\mathbb{I}_{m}&0&0&0&0\\
\\
1&1,\dots,s&\dots&\dots&0&\mathbb{D}&\mathbb{B}_{2}&\dots&\mathbb{B}_{m}\\
\\
2&1,\dots,s&\dots&\dots&0&0&\mathbb{I}_{s}&0&0\\
\\
\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots&0&0&0&\dots&0&\\
\\
m&1,\dots,s&\dots&\dots&0&0&0&0&\mathbb{I}_{s}\\
\end{matrix}}

(5.64)

Table 65:
Jacobian of
{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
with
\ell\in\{1,...,m\}
and
\alpha=\{0,..,s\}
. The first and the second column contain, respectively, all the possible values for the parameters
\ell
and
\alpha
. The third column corresponds to the derivatives of
{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})
with respect to the variables of the vector
{\mathtt{a}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(1)
, and to the Taylor coefficients of the
s
-jet
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}
. The remaining columns contain the derivatives with respect to the coefficients
{\mathtt{p}}_{\mu}
of
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
associated with the families of multi-indices (
5.39
) and (
5.40
), in suitable order.

It is plain to check that matrix (
5.64
) contains a submatrix of maximal rank
ms+m
- corresponding to the derivatives w.r.t. those coefficients associated to the family of multi-indices (
5.39
) - independently of
{\mathtt{a}}
and of the
s
-truncation
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}
on which the
s
-vanishing condition is realized. Hence, since the transformation
\Upsilon
in (
5.29
) is invertible, by Theorem
5.1
the set
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
is determined by
ms+m
linearly independent algebraic equations and has codimension
ms+m
in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
.

As it was the case in the proof of Theorem
5.1
, the same strategy of proof applies for
Z^{j}(r,s,m)
,
j=2,\dots,m
, one just has to switch the rôle of the indices
1
and
j
.

Since
\sigma^{1}(r,s,m):=\Pi_{\mathcal{P}(r,m)}Z^{1}(r,s,m)
(see (
5.14
)) and
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
is algebraic, by the Theorem of Tarski and Seidenberg (see Th.
A.1
)
\sigma^{1}(r,s,m)
is a semi-algebraic set of
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
. Moreover, as Jacobian (
5.64
) has rank
ms+m
w.r.t. the
ms+m
polynomial coefficients associated to the multi-indices of the family (
5.39
), for
\alpha\in\{2,...,s\}
and
i\in\{1,...,m\}
, by Theorem
5.1
and by the implicit function theorem, the conditions
{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
imply

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(i,0)}={\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(i,1)}=0\\
&{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(i,\alpha)}=g_{i\alpha}({\mathtt{p}}_{\mu},{\mathtt{a}},a_{22},...,a_{2s},...,a_{m2},...,a_{ms})\quad,\qquad\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}(\alpha)\end{split}

(5.66)

for some implicit functions
g_{i\alpha}
. That is, one can express the polynomial coefficients
{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(i,0)},{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(i,1)},{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(i,\alpha)}
as implicit functions of the remaining coefficients - associated to the multi-indices in the family
{\mathcal{M}}(\alpha)
defined in (
5.39
) - and of the
(m-1)s
parameters of
{\mathtt{a}}
and
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}
. Moreover, since the functions
{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})
are polynomial for all
\ell\in\{1,...,m\}
and
\alpha\in\{0,..,s\}
, the implicit functions
g_{i\alpha}
are all analytic. Therefore, one has an analytic parametrization of
\sigma^{1}(r,s,m)
given by the
m(s+1)
independent equations (
5.66
), for
i\in\{1,...,m\}
,
\alpha\in\{0,...,s\}
. This, in turn, yields that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{dim}\,\sigma^{1}(r,s,m)=&\dim W^{1}(r,m)-m(s+1)=\dim\mathcal{P}(r,m)+\dim\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)-m(s+1)\\
=&\dim\mathcal{P}(r,m)+(m-1)s-m(s+1)\ ,\end{split}

(5.67)

which implies that the codimension of
\sigma^{1}(r,s,m)
in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{codim}\,\sigma^{1}(r,s,m)=&m(s+1)-(m-1)s=m+s\ .\end{split}

(5.68)

Once again, it is plain to check that the same result holds true also in the case in which the parametrizing coordinate of the curve gamma is the
j
-th, with
j=2,\dots,m
. Hence, one finds
\text{codim }\sigma^{j}(r,s,m)=m+s
for
j=2,\dots,m
, which, together with expression (
5.13
), proves the statement.

∎

5.3
Geometric properties

For fixed integers
r\geq 2
,
m\geq 2
,
1\leq s\leq r-1
, and for any
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, we indicate respectively by
\Sigma(r,s,m):=\overline{\sigma}(r,s,m)
and
\Sigma^{i}(r,s,m):=\overline{\sigma}^{i}(r,s,m)
the closures in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
of the sets
\sigma(r,s,m)
and
{\sigma}^{i}(r,s,m)
introduced in the previous section. Taking (
5.13
) into account, one has

\Sigma(r,s,m)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\Sigma^{i}(r,s,m)\ .

(5.69)

For
m\geq 2
, Corollary
5.1
and Proposition
A.3
ensure that for any
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}

\text{codim }\Sigma^{i}(r,s,m)=\text{ codim }\sigma^{i}(r,s,m)=s+m>0

in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, so that
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\backslash\Sigma^{i}(r,s,m)
is an open set of full Lebesgue measure. Therefore, by (
5.69
), the same holds true also for
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\backslash\Sigma(r,s,m)
.

As we did previously, when
m\geq 2
we only consider the case in which the index of the parametrizing coordinate is
i=1
, as the other cases are immediate generalizations.

In case
m=1
, instead, Lemma
5.1
ensures that

\sigma(r,s,1)=\overline{\sigma}(r,s,1)=:\Sigma(r,s,1)\ .

(5.70)

and

\text{codim }\sigma(r,s,1)=\text{codim }\Sigma(r,s,1)=s+1\ .

(5.71)

Still for
m=1
, in order to make use of uniform notations w.r.t. the case
m=2
and to simplify the exposition in the sequel, we write

\sigma^{1}(r,s,m)\equiv\Sigma^{1}(r,s,1):=\Sigma(r,s,1)

(5.72)

and we extend the notations of subsection
5.2.3
by setting

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}:=\operatorname{id}\quad,\qquad{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}}):=P({\mathtt{y}})\quad,\qquad\text{ for }m=1\ .

(5.73)

The rest of this section will be devoted to the proof of the following

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
m
be a positive integer. For any open set
{\mathtt{D}}\subset\mathcal{P}(r,m)\backslash\Sigma^{1}(r,s,m)
verifying
\overline{{\mathtt{D}}}\cap\Sigma^{1}(r,s,m)=\varnothing
, there exist positive constants
C_{1}({\mathtt{D}})
,
C_{2}(s,m)
such that for any polynomial
P(x)\in{\mathtt{D}}
and for any arc
\gamma\in\Theta_{m}^{1}

one has the following lower estimates

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1,...,m\\
\alpha=1,...,s\end{subarray}}\left|\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})}{\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ\gamma(t)}\right)_{t=0}\right|>C_{1}({\mathtt{D}})\\
&\text{ in case }s=1\text{ or }\lx@nobreakspace m=1,\\
&\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1,...,m\\
\alpha=1,...,s\end{subarray}}\left|\frac{d^{\alpha}}{dt^{\alpha}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}({\mathtt{y}})}{\partial{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}}\right|_{{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ\gamma(t)}\right)_{t=0}\right|>\frac{C_{1}({\mathtt{D}})}{1+C_{2}(s,m)\times\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=2,...,m\\
\alpha=2,...,s\end{subarray}}|a_{\ell\alpha}|}\\
&\text{ in case }2\leq s\leq r-1\text{ and }\ m\geq 2\ ,\end{split}

(5.74)

where - for
m\geq 2
-
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
is the polynomial
P
written in the adapted system of coordinates for
\gamma
introduced in paragraph
5.2.3
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}
is the associated transformation defined in (
5.33
), whereas for
m=1
the symbols
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathtt{a}}
are to be intended as in (
5.73
).

Remark 5.11
.

As we shall see in the next section, for any
\lambda>0
, when
\gamma
is the minimal arc of Theorem
4.1
, one can give a positive upper bound - that only depends on
r,s,m,\lambda
- to the quantity
\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1,...,m\\
\alpha=2,...,s\end{subarray}}|a_{\ell\alpha}|
at the denominator of (
5.74
). This is due to the fact that all minimal arcs satisfy a uniform Bernstein-like inequality on their Taylor coefficients (see formula (
4.1
) in Theorem
4.1
).

Before proving Lemma
5.3
