{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
. To conclude the proof of Case 2, we need the following

Lemma 7.1
.

There exists a sequence
\{S_{k}\in\mathcal{P}(r,n)\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converging to
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)
in
\mathcal{P}(r,n)
and verifying
S_{k}|_{\Gamma^{m}}=P_{k}
for any given
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

We indicate by
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\in{\mathtt{U}}(m,n)
an orthonormal basis of
\Gamma^{m}
, and we choose
n-m
orthonormal supplementary vectors
A_{m+1},\dots,A_{n}
to form a orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In the coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
associated to
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
, the restriction of any polynomial
Q\in\mathcal{P}(r,n)
to the subspace
\Gamma^{m}
is obtained by simply setting
x_{m+1}=\dots=x_{n}=0
in the expression of
Q
. Conversely, any polynomial
P(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
depending only on the first
m
variables is the projection on
\Gamma^{m}
of any polynomial
Q\in\mathcal{P}(r,n)
of the form
Q(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=P(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})+q(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, where
q\in\mathcal{P}(r,n)
verifies
q(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},0)=0
.

By the above discussion and with slight abuse of notation, one can define the polynomial

q_{h}=q_{h}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}):={\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})-{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})

(7.15)

which verifies
q_{h}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},0)=0
by construction.

Then, for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we consider the polynomials

S_{k}=S_{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}):=P_{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})+q_{h}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\ ,

(7.16)

where
\{P_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is the sequence approaching
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
introduced above. The sequence
\{S_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
has the properties we seek. Infact, as
q_{h}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},0)=0
, on the one hand
S_{k}
verifies

S_{k}|_{\Gamma^{m}}=S_{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},0)=P_{k}\qquad\forall\ k\in{\mathbb{N}}\ ;

(7.17)

on the other hand, as
P_{k}\longrightarrow{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
by hypothesis, by taking (
7.15
)-(
7.16
) into account one has

S_{k}\longrightarrow{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)+q_{h}={\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)\ .

(7.18)

∎

Since by Lemma
7.1
one has
S_{k}|_{\Gamma^{m}}=P_{k}
, and since
P_{k}\in\sigma^{1}(r,s_{m},m)
by construction, the same arguments developed at Case 1 yield that for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
the system

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{cases}(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})\in{\mathtt{U}}(m,n)\\[5.0pt]
a(m):=(a_{21},\dots,a_{2s_{m}},\dots,a_{m1},\dots,a_{ms_{m}})\in\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)\times s_{m}}\\[5.0pt]
v=u_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{m}a_{j1}\,u_{j}\\[5.0pt]
(S_{k})^{1}_{0}[v]=(S_{k})^{1}_{0}[u_{2}]=\dots=(S_{k})^{1}_{0}[u_{m}]=0\\[5.0pt]
{\mathcal{H}}_{m,\ell,\alpha}^{S_{k},0}(v,u_{2},\dots,u_{m},a(m,s_{m}))=0\quad i=1,\dots,m\ ,\ \ \lx@nobreakspace\alpha=1,\dots,s\end{cases}\end{split}

(7.19)

must be verified. However, this fact and the fact that, by Lemma
7.1
, one also has
S_{k}\longrightarrow{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)
, contradicts the hypotheses of Theorem B. Hence, we must have
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\not\in\Sigma(r,s_{m},m)\backslash\sigma(r,s_{m},m)
.

By the discussion at Cases 1-2 above, the assumptions of Theorem B imply that - for any
m
-dimensional subspace
\Gamma^{m}
, with
m\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
- the Taylor polynomial
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
lies outside of
\Sigma(r,s_{m},m)
. Hence
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)\in\mathcal{P}(r,n)\backslash{\mathcal{V}}(r,s_{m},m,n)
. This, together with (
6.66
) and Theorem A, concludes the proof.

7.2
Proof of the Corollaries

7.2.1
Proof of Corollary B1

We start by studying the one-dimensional affine subspaces orthogonal to
\nabla h(I_{0})\neq 0
. Hypothesis (
3.18
) is equivalent to hypothesis (
3.15
) in Theorem B with
r=3
,
s_{1}=2
, whence the thesis.

On the other hand, for any fixed
m\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
, since
{\mathtt{G}}(m,n)
and
{\mathbb{S}}^{n}
are both compact, by hypothesis (
3.18
) there exists
\tau_{m}>0
such that for any
m
-dimensional affine subspace
I_{0}+\Gamma^{m}
orthogonal to
\nabla h(I_{0})
, and for any vector
w\in{\mathbb{S}}^{n}\cap\Gamma^{m}
we have

h_{I_{0}}^{1}[w]=0\quad,\qquad|h_{I_{0}}^{2}[w,w]|+|h^{3}_{I_{0}}[w,w,w]|\geq\tau_{m}>0\ .

(7.20)

By continuity, up to dividing the constant
\tau_{m}
by a factor two, relation (
7.20
) holds uniformly also for all polynomials in a small ball around
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,3,n)
. Hence,

h
matches the hypotheses at point
ii)
of Theorem B for
r=3
and
s_{m}=2
, whence the thesis for affine subspaces of dimension higher or equal than two.

7.2.2
Proof of Corollary B2

In the proof of Theorem A we have set
\bm{\Omega}_{n}^{r,{\mathtt{s}}}:=\bigcup_{m=1}^{n-1}{\mathcal{V}}(r,s_{m},m,n)
(see (
6.66
)). Furthermore, formula (
6.2
) ensures that for any given function
h
of class
C^{2r-1}
around
I_{0}

{\mathtt{T}}_{I_{0}}(h,r,n)\in{\mathcal{V}}(r,s_{m},m,n)\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{by definition}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}\quad\begin{subarray}{c}\exists\Gamma^{m}\in{\mathtt{G}}(m,n),\ \Gamma^{m}\perp\nabla h(I_{0})\text{ s.t.}\\
{\mathtt{T}}_{I_{0}}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\Sigma(r,s_{m},m):=\overline{\sigma}(r,s_{m},m)\end{subarray}\ .

(7.21)

Since one is free to choose the order of the orthonormal vectors spanning
\Gamma^{m}
, without any loss of generality we can also write

{\mathtt{T}}_{I_{0}}(h,r,n)\in{\mathcal{V}}(r,s_{m},m,n)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\begin{subarray}{c}\exists\Gamma^{m}\in{\mathtt{G}}(m,n),\ \Gamma^{m}\perp\nabla h(I_{0})\text{ s.t.}\\
{\mathtt{T}}_{I_{0}}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\Sigma^{1}(r,s_{m},m):=\overline{\sigma}^{1}(r,s_{m},m)\end{subarray}\ .

(7.22)

In the proof of Theorem B, we have also seen that for any
m\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, and for any given subspace
\Gamma^{m}\in{\mathtt{G}}(m,n)
orthogonal to
\nabla h(I_{0})
, condition
{\mathtt{T}}_{I_{0}}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\sigma^{1}(r,s_{m},m)
holds if and only if system (
3.19
) (if
m=1
) or (
3.20
) (if
m\geq 2
) admits a solution when
Q
is set to be the Taylor polynomial at order
r
of function
h
.

Hence we have that, for any fixed
m\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, condition

{\mathtt{T}}_{I_{0}}(h,r,n)\in{\mathcal{V}}(r,s_{m},m,n)

is equivalent to asking that
{\mathtt{T}}_{I_{0}}(h,r,n)
belongs to the closure in
\mathcal{P}^{\star}(r,n)
of the set of polynomials solving system (
3.19
) (if
m=1
) or system (
3.20
) (for
m\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
).

The thesis follows by the arguments above and by (
6.66
).

7.2.3
Proof of Corollary B3

By the proof of Theorem B, the hypotheses in
i)
and
ii)
amount to asking for the existence of a real-analytic curve

\gamma(t):=\begin{cases}wt\quad&\text{ for }\lx@nobreakspace m=1\\
\left(t,\sum_{i=1}^{+\infty}a_{2i}t^{i},\dots,\sum_{i=1}^{+\infty}a_{mi}t^{i}\right)\quad&\text{ for }m=\{2,\dots,n-1\}\end{cases}

(7.23)

whose image is contained in some
m
-dimensional subspace orthogonal to
\nabla h(I_{0})\neq 0
, and such that
(\pi_{\Gamma^{m}}\nabla h)\circ\gamma
has a zero of infinite order at
\gamma(0)=I_{0}
. By analyticity, then,
(\pi_{\Gamma^{m}}\nabla h)\circ\gamma
is identically zero. This implies by Definition
1.1
that
h
is not steep.

8
Partition of the set of
s
-vanishing polynomials

The proof of Theorems C1-C2-C3 is quite long and requires intermediate results which will be presented in this section. Before stating them, in the following two paragraphs we introduce some definitions and notations.

8.1
Initial setting

Consider three integers
r,m\geq 2
, and
s\in\{2,\dots,r-1\}
. In sections
5
-
6
, we have indicated by
\Sigma(r,s,m)\subset\mathcal{P}(r,m)
the closure of the set
\sigma(r,s,m)
of
s
-vanishing polynomials. In particular, by (
5.13
)-(
5.14
) one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sigma(r,s,m)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\sigma^{i}(r,s,m)\quad,\qquad\sigma^{i}(r,s,m):=\Pi_{\mathcal{P}(r,m)}Z^{i}(r,s,m)\ ,\\
&\Sigma(r,s,m)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\Sigma^{i}(r,s,m):=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\overline{\sigma}^{i}(r,s,m):=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\overline{\Pi_{\mathcal{P}(r,m)}Z^{i}(r,s,m)}\ ,\end{split}

(8.1)

where the sets
Z^{i}(r,s,m)\subset\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)
, with
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, are defined in (
5.15
), and one has decomposition (
5.16
), namely

\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\vartheta_{m}(s)\supset Z(r,s,m)=:\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}Z^{i}(r,s,m)\ .

The expression of the sets
Z^{i}(r,s,m)
,
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, is given explicitly
15
15

15

Actually, in Theorem
5.1
, only the expression of
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
is explicit. However, as it was already pointed out in section
5
, the cases
i=2,\dots,m
are trivial generalizations of the case
i=1
: in order to find the expression of
Z^{i}(r,s,m)
for
i\neq 1
, one simply has to follow the same steps needed to find the expression for
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
, and to exchange the rôle of the first coordinate with the
i
-th one.
in Theorem
5.1
.

In the previous sections, we have seen that, in order to check if a given polynomial
Q\in\mathcal{P}^{\star}(r,n)
is steep at the origin
16
16

16

It is clear that the arguments devoped in the sequel hold also if the considered point is not the origin.
on a fixed subspace
\Gamma^{m}
orthogonal to
\nabla Q(0)\neq 0
, one must check whether the restriction
P:=Q|_{\Gamma^{m}}\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
belongs to the complementary of
\Sigma(r,s,m):=\overline{\sigma}(r,s,m)
. We now claim that it is not strictly necessary to consider the closure of the whole set
\sigma(r,s,m)
. Indeed, in practice, the curves on which the
s
-vanishing condition must be tested are minimal arcs with uniform characteristics, like the one defined in Theorem
4.1
.

Namely, by the arguments in the proof of Lemma
6.5
- for any given
Q\in\mathcal{P}^{\star}(r,n)
and for any fixed
\Gamma^{m}
perpendicular to
\nabla Q(0)\neq 0
, it is sufficient to check if there exists a threshold
\lambda_{0}>0
such that, for any
0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
, there exists an interval
I_{\lambda}\subset[-\lambda,\lambda]
of length
\lambda/{\mathtt{K}}

17
17

17

More details about the threshold
\lambda_{0}
are given in Lemma
6.5
, whereas the constant
{\mathtt{K}}
is the one introduced in Theorem
4.1
.
, on which - for any curve
\gamma\in\Theta_{m}
verifying the Bernstein’s inequality (
4.1
) - the composition
(\nabla P)\circ\gamma:=(\nabla Q|_{\Gamma^{m}})\circ\gamma
has no zeros of order greater or equal than
s
. In particular, we are interested in testing the
s
-vanishing conditions on those analytic curves
\gamma
over
I_{\lambda}
that, for some
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, verify

\gamma(t)=\left(\begin{matrix}x_{1}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}a_{1k}\,t^{k}\\
\dots\\
x_{i-1}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}a_{{(i-1)}k}\,t^{k}\\
x_{i}(t)=t\\
x_{i+1}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}a_{{(i+1)}k}\,t^{k}\\
\dots\\
x_{m}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}a_{mk}\,t^{k}\\
\end{matrix}\right)\quad,\qquad\max_{u\in I_{\lambda}}\,\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}|a_{jk}(u)|\leq\frac{{\mathtt{M}}(r,n,m,k)}{\lambda^{k-1}}\ .

(8.2)

By Theorem
4.1
, the constant
{\mathtt{M}}={\mathtt{M}}(r,n,m,k)
in (
8.2
) can be explicitly computed and it is uniform for all curves
\gamma\in\Theta_{m}
.

As we have shown in the proof of Lemma
6.5
, for any given
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, the threshold
\lambda_{0}
- if it exists - goes to zero with the distance of
P
to the "bad" set
\sigma(r,s,m)
. Therefore, by formula (
8.2
), the Taylor coefficients of the curves
\gamma
on which the
s
-vanishing condition must be tested may take any value, except for those of order one which, independently from the choice of
\lambda>0
, are always uniformly bounded by
{\mathtt{M}}(r,n,m,1)
.

Inspired by the above reasonings, with the notations in (
8.2
), we give the following

Definition 8.1
.

For
i=1,\dots,m
, we introduce the sets

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{i}:=\left\{\gamma\in\Theta_{m}^{i}|\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\{|a_{j1}(0)|\}\leq{\mathtt{M}}(r,n,m,1)\right\}\\
\end{split}

(8.3)

and

\widehat{\Theta}_{m}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{i}\ ,

(8.4)

and we denote respectively by
\widehat{\vartheta}^{i}_{m}(s)\subset\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s)
and by
\widehat{\vartheta}_{m}(s)\subset\vartheta_{m}(s)
their associated subsets of
s
-truncations.

Remark 8.1
.

By formula (
8.3
), if one introduces the decomposition

\widehat{\vartheta}^{i}_{m}(s)=\widehat{\vartheta}^{i}_{m}(1)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{i}_{m}(s,2)

(8.5)

as in (
5.24
), the space
\widehat{\vartheta}^{i}_{m}(1)
is compact and
\widehat{\vartheta}^{i}_{m}(s,2)\equiv\vartheta^{i}_{m}(s,2)
.

Definition 8.2
.

In section
5.2.3
(see formula (
5.27
)), we defined the set
W^{1}(r,m)
as

W^{1}(r,m):=\mathscr{F}^{1}(\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1})\ ,

(8.6)

where the function
\mathscr{F}^{1}
was introduced in (
5.25
)-(
5.26
).
Similarly, for any
j\in\{2,\dots,m\}
, taking Remark
5.8
into account, we had set

\displaystyle\begin{split}W^{j}(r,m):=\mathscr{F}^{j}(\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1})\ .\end{split}

(8.7)

Now, by (
8.3
) and by the above discussion, for any
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
it makes sense to define also

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{W}^{i}(r,m):=\mathscr{F}^{i}(\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\overline{B}^{m-1}(0,{\mathtt{M}}(r,n,m,1)))\ .\end{split}

(8.8)

We remind that, due to Definition
8.1
and to Remark
5.7
, for any given
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
there exists a polynomial bijection
{\mathfrak{U}}^{i}
between
\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{i}_{m}(1)
and
\widehat{W}^{i}(r,m)
: one is free to work either in the standard coordinates
(p_{\mu},{\mathtt{a}})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)s}
, with
M:=\dim\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, or in the adapted coordinates
({\mathtt{p}}_{\mu},{\mathtt{a}})\in\widehat{W}^{i}(r,m)
.

By the arguments above, without any loss of generality, for any fixed
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
it is sufficient to consider the set of those polynomials
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
verifying the
s
-vanishing condition on the
s
-jet
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\widehat{\vartheta}^{i}_{m}(s)
of some curve
\gamma\in\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{i}
. Namely, following (
5.15
) and (
8.1
), for
i=1,\dots,m
we introduce the semi-algebraic sets

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{Z}^{i}(r,s,m):=&\{(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{i}_{m}(s)\}|(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})\text{ satisfies }\\
&\quad q_{\ell\alpha}^{i}\circ\Phi^{i}(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})=0\text{ for all }\ell\in\{1,...,m\},\alpha\in\{0,...,s\}\}\\
\widehat{Z}(r,s,m):=&\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\widehat{Z}^{i}(r,s,m)\\
\widehat{\sigma}^{i}(r,s,m):=&\Pi_{\mathcal{P}(r,m)}\widehat{Z}^{i}(r,s,m)\quad,\qquad\widehat{\sigma}(r,s,m)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\widehat{\sigma}^{i}(r,s,m)\\
\widehat{\Sigma}(r,s,m):=&\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\Sigma}^{i}(r,s,m):=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\text{closure}\left(\widehat{\sigma}^{i}(r,s,m)\right)\end{split}

(8.9)

and, of course, we have

\widehat{Z}^{i}(r,s,m)\subset Z^{i}(r,s,m)\quad,\qquad\widehat{\sigma}^{i}(r,s,m)\subset\sigma^{i}(r,s,m)\quad\forall\ \lx@nobreakspace i\in\{1,\dots,m\}.

(8.10)

For further convenience, we also state the following simple

Lemma 8.1
.

\widehat{\sigma}(r,s,m)
has codimension
s+m=\text{codim }\sigma(r,s,m)
in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
.

Proof.

By the third line of (
8.9
) and by the second inclusion in (
8.10
), it suffices to prove the statement for
\widehat{\sigma}^{1}(r,s,m)
. By the first line of (
8.9
) and by Definition
8.1
, one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,s,m)=Z^{1}(r,s,m)\bigcap\left\{(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(s)\right\}\ .\end{split}

(8.11)

As it was shown in Corollary
5.1
, the Jacobian associated to the equalities determining
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
has full rank
ms+m
.
Namely, by the discussion below expression (
5.64
), in the adapted coordinates of section
5.2.3
, such a Jacobian has non-zero pivots corresponding to the derivatives w.r.t. the coefficients of the polynomial
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
associated to multi-indices in the family (
5.39
). As we had shown in the proof of Corollary
5.1
, this fact and the Implicit Function Theorem imply that for any pair
(P,{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})
belonging to
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
, one can express
ms+m
coefficients of
P
as implicit functions of the other coefficients of
P
and of the
(m-1)s
parameters of
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}
. This was the argument that led to estimate
\text{codim }\sigma^{1}(r,s,m)=s+m
in Corollary
5.1
. The thesis follows by putting this argument together with formulas (
8.9
)-(
8.11
) and with the fact that
\dim\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)=\dim\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(s)
. ∎

8.2
Partition of
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
and
\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)

Let
r,m\geq 2
be two integers. In this paragraph, we introduce a partition of the spaces
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
and
\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)
which will turn out to be useful in the sequel. In order to do this, we first need to introduce a family of multi-indices.

8.2.1
A family of multi-indices

For
b,c\in\{2,\dots,m\}
,
b\leq c

18
18

18

We have set
b\leq c
in (
8.12
) only for convenience, in order not to have two indices
b,c
corresponding to the same multi-index
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
. Infact, it is clear that if we eliminate this constraint we have
\varpi(b,c)=\varpi(c,b)
for all
b,c\in\{2,\dots,m\}
.
, we set

\varpi(b,c):=\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\ |\ \mu_{1}=0\ ,\ \ \mu_{j}=\delta_{jb}+\delta_{jc}\quad\forall j\in\{2,\dots,m\}\ .

(8.12)

Comparing (
8.12
) with the sub-family
\nu({\mathtt{i}},1)
defined in (
5.39
), it is plain to check that one has the disjoint union

\left(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,c=2\\
b\leq c\end{subarray}}^{m}\{\varpi(b,c)\}\right)\bigsqcup\left(\bigcup_{{\mathtt{i}}=1}^{m}\{\nu({\mathtt{i}},1)\}\right)=\{\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\ |\ |\mu|=2\}\ .

(8.13)

Moreover, we have the following

Lemma 8.2
.

For any polynomial
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, the coefficients
p_{\mu}
associated to the multi-indices
\mu
belonging to the family (
8.12
) are invariant under the transformations of paragraph
5.2.3
. Namely, using the notations in (
5.20
), for any given
{\mathtt{a}}\in\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)
one has

{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(b,c)}=p_{\varpi(b,c)}\qquad\text{ for all }b,c\in\{2,\dots,m\}\quad,\qquad b\leq c\ .

Proof.

We indicate by
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}
the orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
associated to the coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
on which polynomials in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
depend.

For any
{\mathtt{a}}:=(a_{21},\dots,a_{m1})\in\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)
, we also denote by

v_{\mathtt{a}}:=A_{1}+a_{21}A_{2}+a_{31}A_{3}+...+a_{m1}A_{m}\ ,\ \ u_{2}:=A_{2}\ ,\ \ ...\ ,\ \ u_{m}:=A_{m}\ ,

(8.14)

the basis associated to the adapted variables defined in Section
5.2.3
(see (
5.17
)), namely

{\mathtt{y}}_{1}:=x_{1}\quad,\qquad{\mathtt{y}}_{2}={\mathtt{y}}_{2}({\mathtt{a}}):=x_{2}-a_{21}\,x_{1}\quad\dots\quad{\mathtt{y}}_{m}={\mathtt{y}}_{m}({\mathtt{a}}):=x_{m}-a_{m1}\,x_{1}\ .

(8.15)

By (
5.20
), (
5.39
) and (
8.13
), the quadratic terms of the transformed polynomial
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
read

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{cases}{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(1,1)}{\mathtt{y}}_{1}^{2}={\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(1,1)}x_{1}^{2}\\
{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(\ell,1)}{\mathtt{y}}_{1}{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}={\mathtt{p}}_{\nu(\ell,1)}x_{1}(x_{\ell}-a_{\ell 1}x_{1})\quad\ell=2,\dots,m\\
{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(j,\ell)}{\mathtt{y}}_{j}{\mathtt{y}}_{\ell}={\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(j,\ell)}(x_{j}-a_{j1}x_{1})(x_{\ell}-a_{\ell 1}x_{1})\quad j,\ell=2,\dots,m\ ,\ \ \lx@nobreakspace j\leq\ell\ .\end{cases}\end{split}

(8.16)

By expression (
8.16
), we infer that - in the original variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
- for any
j,\ell\in\{2,\dots,m\}
,
j\leq\ell
, the coefficient associated to the monomial
x_{j}x_{\ell}
is
{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(j,\ell)}
, that is

p_{\varpi(j,\ell)}={\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(j,\ell)}\ .

∎

8.2.2
Partition

For any
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, we set

\mathbb{H}^{1}(P):=\left(\begin{matrix}2p_{\varpi(2,2)}&p_{\varpi(2,3)}&\dots&p_{\varpi(2,m)}\\
p_{\varpi(2,3)}&2p_{\varpi(3,3)}&\dots&p_{\varpi(3,m)}\\
\dots&\dots&\ddots&\dots\\
p_{\varpi(2,m)}&p_{\varpi(3,m)}&\dots&2p_{\varpi(m,m)}\end{matrix}\right)

(8.17)

and

{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m):=\{P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)|\ \det\mathbb{H}^{1}(P)\neq 0\}\ .

(8.18)

Remark 8.2
.

Matrix
\mathbb{H}^{1}(P)
is invariant under the transformations of section
5.2.3
. Namely, by Lemma
8.2
, we have

\mathbb{H}^{1}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}):=\left(\begin{matrix}2{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,2)}&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,3)}&\dots&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,m)}\\
{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,3)}&2{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(3,3)}&\dots&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(3,m)}\\
\dots&\dots&\ddots&\dots\\
{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,m)}&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(3,m)}&\dots&2{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(m,m)}\end{matrix}\right)=\mathbb{H}^{1}(P)\ .

(8.19)

We also define

{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{1}(r,m):=\mathcal{P}(r,m)\backslash{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)=\{P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)|\ \det\mathbb{H}^{1}(P)=0\}\ ,

(8.20)

so that we have the disjoint union

\mathcal{P}(r,m)={\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)\bigsqcup{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{1}(r,m)\ .

(8.21)

We now consider the images of
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)
and
{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{1}(r,m)
through the transformation
{\mathfrak{U}}^{1}
defined in Remark
5.7
, namely

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathscr{S}}^{1}_{1}(r,m):={\mathfrak{U}}^{1}\left({\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)\right)=&\left\{({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}})\in\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)|\det\mathbb{H}^{1}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}})\neq 0\right\}\\
{\mathscr{S}}^{1}_{2}(r,m):={\mathfrak{U}}^{1}\left({\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{1}(r,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)\right)=&\left\{({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}})\in\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)\right.|\det\mathbb{H}^{1}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}})=0\Big\}\ .\end{split}

(8.22)

By (
8.22
), we have the partition

\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)={\mathscr{S}}^{1}_{1}(r,m)\bigsqcup{\mathscr{S}}^{1}_{2}(r,m)\ .

(8.23)

Remark 8.3
.

It is clear that the above partition can be implemented also in case one considers adapted variables
({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}})\in\widehat{W}^{i}(r,m)
, with
i\in\{2,\dots,m\}
. By suitably modifying the family of indices in (
8.12
), as well as by introducing an adapted matrix
\mathbb{H}^{i}(P)
, it is possible to define sets
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{i},{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{i}
whose disjoint union yields
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
and sets
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}^{i},{\mathscr{S}}_{2}^{i}
whose disjoint union yields
\widehat{W}^{i}(r,m)
. However, the underlying reasonings are not conceptually different from the ones we did above, therefore we omit them in order not to burden the exposition.

8.3
Two important results

Consider three integers
r,m\geq 2
and
s\in\{1,\dots,r-1\}
. The two results below are the cornerstones of the proof of Theorems C1-C2-C3.

Theorem 8.1
.

In case
r\geq 2,s=1
, for any
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
the semi-algebraic sets
\widehat{\sigma}^{i}(r,1,m)
, and
\widehat{\sigma}(r,1,m)
are closed in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, that is, by formulas (
8.9
),

\widehat{\sigma}^{i}(r,1,m)=\widehat{\Sigma}^{i}(r,1,m)\quad\forall\ i\in\{1,\dots,m\}\quad,\qquad\widehat{\sigma}(r,1,m)=\widehat{\Sigma}(r,1,m)\ .

Moreover, for any
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, taking the definition of transformation
\Upsilon^{i}
into account (see (
5.29
)),
the set

\Pi_{\widehat{W}^{i}(r,m)}\Upsilon^{i}(\widehat{Z}^{i}(r,1,m))

is closed in
\widehat{W}^{i}(r,m)
, and its form can be explicitly computed.

Theorem 8.2
.

For any given values of
r\geq 3
,
s\geq 2
, and
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
there exist two semi-algebraic subsets of
\mathcal{P}(r,m)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&X_{1}^{i}(r,s,m)\subset{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{i}(r,m)\ ,\ \ X_{2}^{i}(r,s,m)\subset{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{i}(r,m)\ ,\end{split}

(8.24)

and two semi-algebraic subsets of
\widehat{W}^{i}(r,m)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Y_{1}^{i}(r,s,m)\subset{\mathscr{S}}_{1}^{i}(r,m)\ ,\ \ Y_{2}^{i}(r,s,m)\subset{\mathscr{S}}_{2}^{i}(r,m)\ ,\ \end{split}

(8.25)

verifying the following properties:

1.

one has the partition

\widehat{\sigma}^{i}(r,s,m)=X_{1}^{i}(r,s,m)\bigsqcup X_{2}^{i}(r,s,m)\ ;

2.

for
j\in\{1,2\}
, one has

X_{j}^{i}(r,s,m)=\Pi_{\mathcal{P}(r,m)}\left(({\mathfrak{U}}^{i})^{-1}(Y_{j}^{i}(r,s,m))\right)\ ,

where
{\mathfrak{U}}^{i}
was defined in Remarks
5.7
-
5.8
;

3.

Y_{1}^{i}(r,s,m)
is closed in
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}^{i}(r,m)
for the topology induced by
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
;

4.

X_{1}^{i}(r,s,m)
is closed in
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{i}(r,m)
for the topology induced by
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
;

5.

the form of
Y_{1}^{i}(r,s,m)
can be explicitly computed starting from the expression of
\widehat{Z}^{i}(r,s,m)
by the means of an algorithm involving only linear operations.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of the above results.

We will only prove Theorems
8.1
-
8.2
in the case
i=1
, as the other cases are simple generalizations.

8.3.1
Proof of Theorem
8.1
and strategy of proof of Theorem
8.2
.

We have seen in section
5
that the equations determining
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
, can be written in the adapted coordinates introduced in section
5.2.3
. Namely, by recalling the functions

{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}):W^{1}(r,m)\times\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)(s-1)}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\quad,\qquad{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}\quad\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}\ ,

(8.26)

presented in (
5.36
)-(
5.37
), Theorem
5.1
ensures that
Z^{1}(r,s,m)
is the image through the inverse of the transformation
\Upsilon^{1}
in (
5.30
) of the zero set

\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}\end{subarray}}\{({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})\in W^{1}(r,m)\times\mathbb{R}^{(m-1)(s-1)}|{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0\}\ ,

(8.27)

and the explicit form of the quantities
{\mathtt{Q}}_{i\alpha}
is given in (
5.43
)-(
5.44
).

Then, if we indicate by
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}
the restriction of
{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}
to the subset
\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)\subset W^{1}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
, and if we denote by
{\mathscr{N}}\left(\{\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}\end{subarray}}\right)
the zero set of the non-linear maps
\{\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}\end{subarray}}
, it is clear by the discussion at paragraph
8.1
, in particular by (
8.11
), that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,s,m):=(\Upsilon^{1})^{-1}\left({\mathscr{N}}\left(\{\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}\end{subarray}}\right)\right)\\
\end{split}

(8.28)

that is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,s,m))={\mathscr{N}}\left(\{\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}\end{subarray}}\right)\ .\end{split}

(8.29)

Proof.

(Theorem
8.1
)

When
s=1
, then in (
5.43
)-(
5.44
) one must consider
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
. We observe that

\forall\ \alpha\in\{0,1\}\ ,\ \ \lx@nobreakspace\lx@nobreakspace\lx@nobreakspace\forall{\mathtt{i}}=1,\dots,m\qquad\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu({\mathtt{i}},\alpha)}=0\ ,

(8.30)

where the family of multi-indices
\nu({\mathtt{i}},\alpha)
was defined in (
5.39
). As we see, no parameters belonging to the space
\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
appear in equation (
8.30
), which determines a closed subset of
\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)
. In other words, by (
8.29
) with
s=1
, we have that the projection
\Pi_{\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)}\left(\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,1,m))\right)

is closed in
\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)
and its form is given in (
8.30
). Moreover, as the function
{\mathfrak{U}}^{1}
introduced in Remark
5.7
is continuous by construction, the inverse image
({\mathfrak{U}}^{1})^{-1}\left(\Pi_{\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)}\left(\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,1,m))\right)\right)
is closed in
\mathcal{P}(r,n)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)
. Finally, as all the elements of
\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)
live in a compact ball (see (
8.3
) and Remark (
8.1
)), using Lemma
E.1
with
E\equiv\mathcal{P}(r,m)
and
K\equiv\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)
and taking formulas (
8.9
)-(
8.29
) into account one has that

\widehat{\sigma}^{1}(r,1,m)=\Pi_{\mathcal{P}(r,m)}\left[({\mathfrak{U}}^{1})^{-1}\left(\Pi_{\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)}\left(\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,1,m))\right)\right)\right]

(8.31)

is closed. This concludes the proof.
∎

Now, taking (
8.29
), the third line of (
8.9
) and the proof of Theorem
8.1
into account, the key idea behind the proof of Theorem
8.2
consists in understanding under which conditions the set
\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,s,m))
admits a closed projection onto
\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)
.

In particular, we will see that if we only consider polynomials in the set
{\mathsf{S}}^{1}_{1}(r,m)\subset\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, it is possible to extrapolate linearly from the
ms+m
equations in (
8.29
) the
(m-1)s
parameters of
\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
as explicit algebraic functions of the parameters of
{\mathscr{S}}^{1}_{1}(r,m):={\mathfrak{U}}^{1}({\mathsf{S}}^{1}_{1}(r,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1))
, as the discussion at the following paragraph shows.

We start by setting

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathsf{A}}(r,s,m):=&{\mathscr{N}}\left(\{\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}\end{subarray}}\right)\bigcap\Upsilon^{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,s,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(s))\ ,\end{split}

(8.32)

and we remind that, by Remark
5.7
,

\Upsilon^{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,s,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(s))={\mathfrak{U}}^{1}\left({\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,s,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)\right)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)={\mathscr{S}}^{1}_{1}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)\ .

(8.33)

We now claim that
{\mathsf{A}}(r,s,m)
is a graph of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathsf{A}}(r,s,m)=Y^{1}_{1}(r,s,m)\times\overline{\theta}(r,s,m)\ ,\end{split}

(8.34)

where

Y^{1}_{1}(r,s,m):=\Pi_{\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)}{\mathsf{A}}(r,s,m)

is a closed subset of
{\mathscr{S}}^{1}_{1}(r,m)
for the induced topology determined by algebraic equations involving the coordinates of elements in
{\mathscr{S}}^{1}_{1}(r,m)
, and the points of
\overline{\theta}(r,s,m)\subset\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
are parametrized by
Y^{1}_{1}(r,s,m)
. In the following subparagraphs, we will prove this claim and show how it can be used in order to demonstrate Theorem
8.2
.

8.3.2
Explicit form of the equations (case
r\geq 3
,
s\geq 2
)

The goal of this paragraph is to give a more explicit expression of
{\mathscr{N}}\left(\{\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
\alpha\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\right)
, in case
r\geq 3
,
s\geq 2
.

Remark 8.4
.

We remind that equations (
5.43
)-(
5.44
) are recursive w.r.t. the parameters of the curve
\gamma
. Namely, for any given integer
\beta\in\{2,\dots,s\}
, the coefficients of order
\beta
belonging to the space
\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
- that is
a_{22},\dots,a_{2\beta},\dots,a_{m2},\dots,a_{m\beta}
- appear in equations (
5.43
)-(
5.44
) only for
\alpha\geq\beta
.

For any polynomial
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
and any curve
\gamma\in\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{1}
, we indicate by

{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)}

the associated polynomial
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
written in the adapted coordinates for
\gamma
(see paragraph
5.2.3
) deprived of its monomials of degree less or equal than two
19
19

19

One has
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)}\neq 0
since we are considering the case of polynomials having degree
r\geq 3
.
. Also, if
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)
is the
s
-truncation of
\gamma
, for any given
\alpha\in\{2,\dots,s\}
we denote by

{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}(t):={\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}-\left(\begin{matrix}0\\
\sum_{i=\alpha}^{s}a_{2i}t^{i}\\
\dots\\
\sum_{j=\alpha}^{s}a_{mj}t^{j}\\
\end{matrix}\right)

its truncation at order
\alpha-1
written in the adapted coordinates for
\gamma
.

Remark 8.5
.

We observe that, for
\alpha=2
,
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<2)}(t)
reduces to the line
(t,0,\dots,0)
, since with the exception of the parametrizing coordinate, the components of
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}
start at order two in
t
(see paragraph
5.2.3
).

With this setting, we have

Lemma 8.3
.

For any polynomial
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, there exists a linear change of coordinates
{\mathfrak{D}}={\mathfrak{D}}(P):\mathbb{R}^{m}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that for any given
\alpha\in\{2,\dots,s\}
, and for
{\mathtt{i}}=1,\dots,m
, the algebraic equations
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
in (
5.43
)-(
5.44
) take the form

\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&0&\dots&0\\
0&2{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime}_{\varpi(2,2)}&0&\dots&0\\
0&0&2{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime}_{\varpi(3,3)}&\dots&0\\
0&\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
0&0&0&\dots&2{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime}_{\varpi(m,m)}\\
\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}0\\
a^{\prime}_{2\alpha}\\
a^{\prime}_{3\alpha}\\
\dots\\
a^{\prime}_{m\alpha}\end{matrix}\right)+{\mathfrak{D}}\left(\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{1\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\\
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{2\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\\
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{3\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\\
\dots\\
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{m\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\end{matrix}\right)=0\ .

(8.35)

Proof.

Step 1.
We firstly claim that equations (
5.43
)-(
5.44
) can be but into the form

\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&0&\dots&0\\
0&2{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,2)}&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,3)}&\dots&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,m)}\\
0&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,3)}&2{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(3,3)}&\dots&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(3,m)}\\
0&\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
0&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,m)}&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(3,m)}&\dots&2{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(m,m)}\\
\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}0\\
a_{2\alpha}\\
a_{3\alpha}\\
\dots\\
a_{m\alpha}\end{matrix}\right)+\left(\begin{matrix}\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{1\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\\
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{2\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\\
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{3\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\\
\dots\\
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{m\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\end{matrix}\right)=0\ .

(8.36)

By (
8.30
), for any
\alpha\in\{2,\dots,s\}
, and for any
{\mathtt{i}},{\mathtt{j}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, the monomials
{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu({\mathtt{j}},0)},{\mathtt{p}}_{\nu({\mathtt{j}},1)}
do not yield any contribution to equations
{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
. Therefore, taking (
8.13
) into account, the only monomials of order two which may appear in equation
{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
are those associated to the multi-indices
\varpi(b,c)
, with
b,c\in\{2,\dots,m\}
,
b\leq c
.

Moreover, fixing the values of
{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and
\alpha\in\{2,\dots,s\}
, by (
5.43
)-(
5.44
), the multi-indices
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
contributing to equation
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
are those for which the set
{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(\widetilde{\mu}({\mathtt{i}}),\alpha)
in (
5.41
) is non-empty. This amounts to requiring that the components of the vector
(k_{22},\dots,k_{2\alpha},\dots,k_{m2},\dots,k_{m\alpha})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{(m-1)(\alpha-1)}
appearing in formulas (
5.43
)-(
5.44
) satisfy

\sum_{i=2}^{\alpha}k_{ji}=\widetilde{\mu}_{j}({\mathtt{i}})\quad\forall j\in\{2,\dots,m\}\quad,\qquad\widetilde{\mu}_{1}({\mathtt{i}})+\sum_{j=2}^{m}\sum_{i=2}^{\alpha}i\ k_{ji}=\alpha\ .

(8.37)

In particular, for fixed
{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
\alpha\in\{2,\dots,s\}
and
\ell\in\{2,\dots,m\}
, if we look at the monomials containing the coefficient
a_{\ell\alpha}
in equation
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
- that is, at the form of the terms for which
k_{\ell\alpha}\neq 0
in (
5.43
)-(
5.44
) - by (
8.37
) we must have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{\mu}_{1}({\mathtt{i}})=0\quad,\qquad k_{ji}=\delta_{i\alpha}\delta_{j\ell}\quad,\qquad\widetilde{\mu}_{j}({\mathtt{i}})=\delta_{j\ell}\quad,\qquad j\in\{2,\dots,m\}\ .\end{split}

(8.38)

Firstly, expression (
8.38
) implies
|\mu|=2
. Secondly, as we said above, the only multi-indices of length
|\mu|=2
which may yield a contribution to
{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
are those belonging to the family
\{\varpi(b,c)\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,c\in\{2,\dots,m\}b\leq c\end{subarray}}
in (
8.12
). Therefore, we have two cases.

Case
{\mathtt{i}}=1
.
If
\mu\in\{\varpi(b,\ell)\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,\ell\in\{2,\dots,m\}b\leq\ell\end{subarray}}
then
\mu_{1}=0
, and all terms in formula (
5.43
) which are associated to these indices are multiplied by zero. Hence, the coefficients
a_{\ell\alpha}
do not appear in
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{1\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
, nor do any of the monomials of order two in
{\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}
. This, together with Remark
8.4
proves the claim for
{\mathtt{i}}=1
(the first line of (
8.36
)).

Case
{\mathtt{i}}\in\{2,\dots,m\}
.
Taking (
8.38
) into account, for any given
\ell\in\{2,\dots,m\}
one has that

1.

if
{\mathtt{i}}\leq\ell
, the only term to which the coefficient
a_{\ell\alpha}
is associated in equation
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
is the one corresponding to the multi-index
\mu\in\varpi({\mathtt{i}},\ell)
, that is, by (
5.44
), the monomial
(1+\delta_{\ell{\mathtt{i}}}){\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi({\mathtt{i}},\ell)}a_{\ell\alpha}
;

2.

if
{\mathtt{i}}>\ell
, the term containing
a_{\ell\alpha}
is the one associated to the multi-index
\mu\in\varpi(\ell,{\mathtt{i}})
, that is, by (
5.44
),
(1+\delta_{\ell{\mathtt{i}}}){\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(\ell,{\mathtt{i}})}a_{\ell\alpha}
.

Conversely, if a monomial associated to an index
\varpi({\mathtt{i}},\ell)
, with
{\mathtt{i}},\ell\in\{2,\dots,m\}
,
{\mathtt{i}}\leq\ell
, appears in equations
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
, then, by (
8.12
) and by (
8.37
), one must necessarily have

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\widetilde{\mu}_{1}({\mathtt{i}})+\sum_{j=2}^{m}\sum_{i=2}^{\alpha}i\ k_{ji}=\alpha\quad,\qquad\widetilde{\mu}_{1}({\mathtt{i}})=0\\
\sum_{i=2}^{\alpha}k_{ji}=\widetilde{\mu}_{j}({\mathtt{i}}):=\delta_{j\ell}+\delta_{j{\mathtt{i}}}-\delta_{j{\mathtt{i}}}=\delta_{j\ell}\quad\forall j\in\{2,\dots,m\}\ ,\end{cases}

(8.39)

which is true if and only if for some
v\in\{2,\dots,\alpha\}
one has

\displaystyle\begin{cases}k_{ji}=\delta_{j\ell}\delta_{iv}\\
\sum_{j=2}^{m}\sum_{i=2}^{\alpha}i\ \delta_{j\ell}\delta_{iv}=\alpha\end{cases}

(8.40)

that is if and only if
k_{ji}=\delta_{j\ell}\delta_{i\alpha}
.
One can check by formula (
5.44
) that this ensures that such a term must be of the form
(1+\delta_{\ell{\mathtt{i}}}){\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi({\mathtt{i}},\ell)}a_{\ell\alpha}
. This reasoning, together with Remark
8.4
, and with the fact that - as we showed at the beginning of the proof - no monomials of order two appear in equation
\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0
other than those associated to the family (
8.12
), proves the claim for
{\mathtt{i}}\in\{2,\dots,m\}
(rows
2,\dots,m
of (
8.36
)).

Step 2.
For any
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, taking Lemma
8.2
into account we indicate by
\mathbb{G}^{1}(P)
the matrix

\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&\dots&0\\
0&2p_{\varpi(2,2)}&\dots&p_{\varpi(2,m)}\\
0&\dots&\ddots&\dots\\
0&p_{\varpi(2,m)}&\dots&2p_{\varpi(m,m)}\\
\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&\dots&0\\
0&2{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,2)}&\dots&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,m)}\\
0&\dots&\ddots&\dots\\
0&{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(2,m)}&\dots&2{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(m,m)}\\
\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&\mathbb{H}^{1}(P)\end{matrix}\right)

(8.41)

appearing in (
8.36
).

\mathbb{G}^{1}(P)
is symmetric, hence diagonalizable. Hence, for any
P\in\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, there exist a basis of eigenvectors, indicated by

v_{\mathtt{a}},\,u^{\prime}_{2}={\mathtt{u}}^{\prime}_{2}(P),\dots,\,u^{\prime}_{m}=u^{\prime}_{m}(P)\ ,

(8.42)

and a real
m\times m
invertible matrix
{\mathfrak{D}}={\mathfrak{D}}(P)
, such that equation (
8.35
) takes the form

\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&0&\dots&0\\
0&2{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime}_{\varpi(2,2)}&0&\dots&0\\
0&0&2{\mathtt{p}}_{\varpi(3,3)}^{\prime}&\dots&0\\
0&\dots&\dots&\dots&\dots\\
0&0&0&\dots&2{\mathtt{p}}^{\prime}_{\varpi(m,m)}\\
\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}0\\
a^{\prime}_{2\alpha}\\
a^{\prime}_{3\alpha}\\
\dots\\
a^{\prime}_{m\alpha}\end{matrix}\right)+{\mathfrak{D}}\left(\begin{matrix}{\mathtt{Q}}_{1\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\\
{\mathtt{Q}}_{2\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\\
{\mathtt{Q}}_{3\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\\
\dots\\
{\mathtt{Q}}_{m\alpha}\left({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}}^{(>2)},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}^{(<\alpha)}\right)\end{matrix}\right)=0\ ,

(8.43)

where the primed quantities indicate that we are working in the new basis (
8.42
).

Remark 8.6
.

Comparing (
8.14
) with (
8.42
), we observe that the vector
v_{\mathtt{a}}
was left unchanged. This is due to the fact that, by (
8.41
),
v_{\mathtt{a}}
is already an eigenvector of
\mathbb{G}^{1}(P)
(associated to a null eigenvalue).

∎

8.3.3
Proof of Theorem
8.2

For
r\geq 3
,
s\geq 2
, we define

X_{1}^{1}(r,s,m):=\widehat{\sigma}^{1}(r,s,m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)\ .

(8.44)

Step 1.
Firstly, we show that, if
P\in{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)
, then the parameters of the space
\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
can be reduced iteratively from equation (
8.35
) for
\alpha\in\{2,\dots,s\}
.

When
\alpha=2
the second term at the l.h.s. of formula (
8.35
) in Lemma
8.3.2
does not depend on the parameters of
\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
(see Remark
8.5
), so that the coefficients
a_{22},\dots,a_{m2}
can be reduced, as matrix
\mathbb{H}^{1}(P)
is invertible by construction when
P\in{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)
.

If, for
\alpha\in\{3,\dots,s\}
, we assume that the parameters
a_{j\beta}
, with
j\in\{2,\dots,m\}
and
\beta\in\{2,\dots,\alpha-1\}
, have been reduced, then the first equation in (
8.35
) does not contain any new parameter, whereas the terms
a_{2\alpha},\dots,a_{m\alpha}
can be found by inverting
\mathbb{H}^{1}(P)
once again.

The above considerations and (
8.44
) imply that if
P\in X_{1}^{1}(r,s,m)
then

1.

the parameters of the space
\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
can be reduced from the equations in (
8.35
) by the means of a recursive algorithm which only involves linear computations and the inversion of
\mathbb{H}^{1}(P)
;

2.

there exists a truncation
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}\in\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s)
such that
{\mathfrak{U}}^{1}(P\times{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}})=({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})
solves (
8.35
).

Taking (
8.32
) and the above arguments into account, with the notations of section
8.3.1
we have that
{\mathsf{A}}(r,s,m)
is determined by a system of
ms+m-(m-1)(s-1)=s+2m-1
explicit algebraic equations involving only the coordinates of
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)
, and of
(m-1)(s-1)
explicit algebraic equations that parametrize the coefficients of
\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
as functions of the points in
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)
. In other words,
{\mathsf{A}}(r,s,m)
has the form of a graph of the type indicated in (
8.34
), and
Y^{1}_{1}(r,s,m)
is a closed algebraic subset of
{\mathscr{S}}^{1}_{1}(r,m)
whose form can be explicitly computed - starting from the expression of
\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,s,m))
- by the means of an algorithm involving only linear operations.

Moreover, by Remark
5.7
the function
{\mathfrak{U}}^{1}
is polynomial, and
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,s,m)
is obviously semi-algebraic in
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
by construction. Therefore, by (
8.32
)-(
8.33
) the set
{\mathsf{A}}(r,s,m)
is semi-algebraic in
\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)\times\vartheta^{1}_{m}(s,2)
, and
Y^{1}_{1}(r,s,m)
is semi-algebraic in
\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)
by the Theorem of Tarski and Seidenberg
A.1
.

Step 2.
Since the invertible transformation
{\mathfrak{U}}^{1}
defined in Remark
5.7
is polynomial, due to Step 1 (see (
8.32
)-(
8.33
)-(
8.34
)) and to continuity we have that the inverse image

({\mathfrak{U}}^{1})^{-1}(Y^{1}_{1}(r,s,m))\subset{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)

(8.45)

is closed in
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)
for the induced topology. Finally by taking (
8.32
)-(
8.34
)-(
8.44
) into account, and by considering the fact that, as we have already pointed out in Remark
8.1
,
\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)
is compact - Lemma
E.1
ensures that the projection

\Pi_{\mathcal{P}(r,m)}\left(({\mathfrak{U}}^{1})^{-1}(Y^{1}_{1}(r,s,m))\right)=X^{1}_{1}(r,s,m)=\widehat{\sigma}^{1}(r,s,m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)

(8.46)

is closed in
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,s,m)
for the topology induced by
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
.

The semi-algebraicness of the projection in (
8.46
) is a consequence of the semi-algebraicness of
Y^{1}_{1}(r,s,m)
demonstrated at Step 1, of the fact that
{\mathfrak{U}}^{1}
is polynomial, and of the Theorem of Tarski and Seidenberg.

The above arguments prove Theorem
8.2
once one sets

\begin{split}&X_{2}^{1}(r,s,m):=\widehat{\sigma}^{1}(r,s,m)\backslash X_{1}^{1}(r,s,m)\\
\lx@nobreakspace&Y_{2}^{1}(r,s,m):=\Pi_{\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)}\left({\mathscr{N}}\left(\{\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s\}\end{subarray}}\right)\right)\backslash Y_{1}^{1}(r,s,m)\ .\end{split}

(8.47)

9
Proof of Theorems C1-C2-C3

We assume the notations of section
8
. In this section, we consider two positive integers
r\geq 2
,
n\geq 3
, a vector
{\mathtt{s}}:=(s_{1},\dots,s_{n-1})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n-1}
, with
1\leq s_{i}\leq r-1
for all
i=1,\dots,n-1
, and a function
h
of class
C_{b}^{2r-1}
around the origin, verifying
\nabla h(0)\neq 0
.

Also, for any
m\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
, we set
{\mathscr{K}}(r,n,m):={\mathtt{M}}(r,n,m,1)
, where the constant
{\mathtt{M}}(r,n,m,1)
is the one appearing in the statement of Theorem
4.1
(see formula (
4.1
)).

9.1
Proof of Theorem C1

Fix
m\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
. Let
\Gamma^{m}
be a given
m
-dimensional subspace belonging to the subset
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{0}(h,m,n)\subset{\mathtt{G}}(m,n)
introduced in Definition
3.2
.

Taking Definition
8.3
into account, we consider a curve
\gamma\in\widehat{\Theta}_{m}
whose image is contained in
\Gamma^{m}
. Without any loss of generality, up to changing the order of the vectors spanning
\Gamma^{m}
, we can suppose that
\gamma
is parametrized by the first coordinate, hence that
\gamma\in\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{1}
. Following (
8.2
), we indicate by
{\mathtt{a}}=(a_{21},\dots,a_{m1})\in\overline{B}^{m-1}(0,{\mathscr{K}}(r,n,m))
the linear coefficients coefficients of the Taylor expansion of
\gamma
at the origin, and by
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}
its
s
-truncation (with
1\leq s\leq r-1
).

We also indicate by
u_{1},\dots,u_{m}\in{\mathtt{U}}(m,n)
a orthonormal basis spanning
\Gamma^{m}
, and by
v:=u_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{m}a_{i1}u_{i},u_{2}\dots,u_{m}
the basis associated to the adapted coordinates for
\gamma
introduced in section
5.2.3
. As we have already shown in (
7.3
), the Taylor polynomial
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)
restricted to
\Gamma^{m}
written in the adapted coordinates reads

{\mathtt{T}}_{0,{\mathtt{a}}}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)({\mathtt{y}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
1\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\mu!}h_{0}^{|\mu|}\Big[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{1}}}{{\overbrace{v}}},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{2}}}{{\overbrace{u_{2}}}},\dots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{m}}}{{\overbrace{u_{m}}}}\Big]\,{\mathtt{y}}_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\dots{\mathtt{y}}_{m}^{\mu_{m}}\ ,

(9.1)

where we have used the notation introduced in formula (
2.2
). Moreover, as customary,
{{\mathcal{J}}_{s,\gamma}}
reads
{\mathtt{J}_{s,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}
in the new coordinates.

By the arguments at paragraph
8.1
, and by taking (
5.29
) and (
8.9
) into account, if we manage to prove that condition

({\mathtt{T}}_{0,{\mathtt{a}}}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m),{\mathtt{a}},{{\mathtt{J}}_{1,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})\in\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,1,m))

is never satisfied for any choice of the subspace
\Gamma^{m}\in{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{0}(h,m,n)
and of the curve
\gamma
, which is equivalent - due to Theorem
8.1
for
s=1
- to condition

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\not\in\widehat{\sigma}^{1}(r,1,m)=\widehat{\Sigma}^{1}(r,1,m)\quad,\qquad\forall\ \Gamma^{m}\in{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{0}(h,m,n)\ ,

(9.2)

then by the definitions in (
6.1
)-(
6.2
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n)\in\qquad&\\
\mathcal{P}^{\star}(r,n)\backslash\Pi_{\mathcal{P}^{\star}(r,n)}\{&(Q,A,P)\in\mathcal{P}^{\star}(r,n)\times O(n,m)\times\mathcal{P}(r,m)|\\
&\text{Span }(A)\in{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{0}\ ,\ \ P(x)=Q(Ax)\ ,\ \ P(x)\in\Sigma(r,1,m)\}\ .\end{split}

(9.3)

Hence, if claim (
9.2
) is true, taking (
9.3
) into account one retraces exactly the same steps used to demonstrate Theorem A, (see section
6
), and the thesis follows. As it has already been pointed out in subparagraph
3.2.4
, one just needs to be careful about the fact that the steepness coefficients, in this case, do not admit a uniform lower bound, as they may tend to zero when a subspace not belonging to
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{0}(h,m,n)
and on which
h
is not steep is approached. Moreover, one cannot expect to have steepness on the subspaces of
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{0}(h,m,n)
for an open set of functions around
h
.

For any choice of
\Gamma^{m}\in{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{0}(h,m,n)
, by Theorem
5.1
and by (
5.37
), condition

({\mathtt{T}}_{0,{\mathtt{a}}}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m),{\mathtt{a}},{{\mathtt{J}}_{1,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})\not\in\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,1,m))

is equivalent to asking that system

{\mathtt{Q}}_{i\alpha}({\mathtt{T}}_{0,{\mathtt{a}}}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m),{\mathtt{a}},{{\mathtt{J}}_{1,\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0\qquad\forall\ i\in\{1,\dots,m\}\ ,\ \ \forall\alpha\in\{0,1\}\ ,

(9.4)

has no solution.
Then, by expressions (
7.8
)-(
7.9
), the system in (
9.4
) is not satisfied if and only if

\begin{cases}(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})\in{\mathtt{U}}(m,n)\quad,\qquad\text{ Span }(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})=\Gamma^{m}\\
(a_{21},\dots,a_{m1})\in\overline{B}^{m-1}(0,{\mathscr{K}}(r,n,m))\quad,\qquad v:=u_{1}+a_{21}u_{2}+\dots+a_{m1}u_{m}\\
h_{0}^{1}[v]=h_{0}^{1}[u_{2}]=\dots=h^{1}_{0}[u_{m}]\\
h^{2}_{0}[v,v]=h^{2}_{0}[v,u_{2}]=\dots=h^{2}_{0}[v,u_{m}]=0\end{cases}

(9.5)

has no solution.

By construction, the Hessian of the restriction
h|_{\Gamma^{m}}
has no null eigenvalues, so that system (
9.5
) admits no solution, and both claim (
9.2
) and expression (
9.3
) hold true, as wished. This concludes the proof.

9.2
Proof of Theorem C2

9.2.1
Case of a subspace belonging to
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{1}(h,m,n)

With the usual setting, let
m\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
be an integer, and
\Gamma^{m}\in{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{1}(h,m,n)\subset{\mathtt{G}}(m,n)
be a
m
-dimensional subspace spanned by vectors
u_{1},\dots,u_{m}\in{\mathtt{U}}(m,n)
.

As the Hessian matrix of the restriction
h|_{\Gamma^{m}}
has at most one null eigenvalue, without any loss of generality one can choose
u_{1}
to be the eigenvector associated to the unique null eigenvalue, that is

\begin{cases}h^{1}_{0}[u_{1}]=h^{1}_{0}[u_{2}]=\dots=h_{0}^{1}[u_{m}]=0\\
h_{0}^{2}[u_{1},u_{1}]=h^{2}_{0}[u_{1},u_{2}]=h^{2}_{0}[u_{1},u_{m}]=0\\
\text{Span}(u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{m})=\Gamma^{m}\end{cases}

(9.6)

so that at the same time one must have

\det\left(\begin{matrix}h^{2}_{0}[u_{2},u_{2}]&h^{2}_{0}[u_{2},u_{3}]&\dots&h^{2}_{0}[u_{2},u_{m}]\\
h^{2}_{0}[u_{3},u_{2}]&h^{2}_{0}[u_{3},u_{3}]&\dots&h^{2}_{0}[u_{3},u_{m}]\\
\dots\\
h^{2}_{0}[u_{m},u_{2}]&h^{2}_{0}[u_{m},u_{3}]&\dots&h^{2}_{0}[u_{m},u_{m}]\\
\end{matrix}\right)\neq 0\ .

(9.7)

The expression of
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
w.r.t. the coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
associated to the vectors
u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{m}
reads

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\\
1\leq|\mu|\leq r\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\mu!}h_{0}^{|\mu|}\Big[\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{1}}}{{\overbrace{u_{1}}}},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{2}}}{{\overbrace{u_{2}}}},\dots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{m}}}{{\overbrace{u_{m}}}}\Big]\,x_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\dots x_{m}^{\mu_{m}}\ .

(9.8)

We now claim that

Lemma 9.1
.

If
h
is non-steep at the origin on
\Gamma^{m}
at some given order
s_{m}\geq 2
, then

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in X_{1}^{1}(r,s_{m},m):=\widehat{\sigma}^{1}(r,s_{m},m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)

(9.9)

and
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
satisfies the
s_{m}
-vanishing condition on
\Gamma^{m}
on some curve
\gamma\in\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{1}
whose Taylor expansion at the origin has null linear terms.

Proof.

Looking at (
9.8
), it is easy to check that the coefficients of
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
associated to the family of indices

\{\varpi(b,c)\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}b,c\in\{2,\dots,m\},b\leq c\end{subarray}}

introduced in (
8.12
) read

p_{\varpi(b,c)}=\left({\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\right)_{\varpi(b,c)}=\frac{1}{1+\delta_{bc}}h^{2}_{0}[u_{b},u_{c}]\ ,

(9.10)

where
\delta_{bc}
is the Kronecker delta.

By putting together expressions (
9.7
) - (
9.10
) with the definition of set
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)
in (
8.18
), one has that

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)\ .

(9.11)

Since we have assumed
h
is non-steep at the origin on
\Gamma^{m}
at some order
s_{m}\geq 2
then, by the discussions at section
6
and at paragraph
8.1
, one has

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\Sigma}(r,s_{m},m)

(9.12)

which, together with (
9.11
), yields

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\Sigma}(r,s_{m},m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)\ .

(9.13)

We now claim that

Lemma 9.2
.

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\not\in\widehat{\Sigma}^{i}(r,s_{m},m)\qquad\forall\ i\in\{2,\dots,m\}\ .

(9.14)

Proof.

Suppose, by absurd, that
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\Sigma}^{i}(r,s_{m},m)
for some
i\in\{2,\dots,m\}
. It is clear from Theorem
5.1
and (
8.9
) that, as
s_{m}\geq 2
, one has
\widehat{\sigma}^{i}(r,s_{m},m)\subset\widehat{\sigma}^{i}(r,1,m)
, and therefore
\widehat{\Sigma}^{i}(r,s_{m},m)\subset\widehat{\Sigma}^{i}(r,1,m)
. This fact and the initial hypothesis imply
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\Sigma}^{i}(r,1,m)
, so that by Theorem
8.1
, one must have

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\sigma}^{i}(r,1,m)\ .

(9.15)

Relation (
9.15
) implies that there must exist a curve
\gamma\in\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{i}
with values in
\Gamma^{m}
such that
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
satisfies the
1
-vanishing condition on
\gamma
. As it was shown in the proof of Theorem C1 (see the discussion around formula (
9.4
)) this is equivalent to asking that system

\begin{cases}h^{1}_{0}[u_{1}]=h^{1}_{0}[u_{2}]=\dots=h^{1}_{0}[u_{i}]=\dots=h^{1}_{0}[u_{m}]=0\\
h^{2}_{0}[u_{i},u_{1}]=h^{1}_{0}[u_{i},u_{2}]=\dots=h^{1}_{0}[u_{i},u_{i}]=\dots=h^{1}_{0}[u_{i},u_{m}]=0\end{cases}\qquad i\neq 1

(9.16)

admits a solution, which contradicts (
9.7
).
∎

Due to (
9.13
) and to Lemma
9.2
, we then have that

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\Sigma}^{1}(r,s_{m},m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)\ .

(9.17)

Moreover, by Theorem
8.2
, the set
X_{1}^{1}(r,s,m):=\widehat{\sigma}^{1}(r,s,m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)
defined in (

) is closed in
{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)
for the topology induced by
\mathcal{P}(r,m)
, whence one deduces that actually

X_{1}^{1}(r,s_{m},m)=\text{closure}\left(\widehat{\sigma}^{1}(r,s_{m},m)\right)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)=\widehat{\Sigma}^{1}(r,s_{m},m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)\ .

(9.18)

Relations (
9.17
) and (
9.18
) together imply

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in X_{1}^{1}(r,s_{m},m)\ .

(9.19)

Therefore, by (
9.19
) and by the definition of
X^{1}_{1}(r,s_{m},m)
in (
8.44
), there exists a curve
\gamma\in\widehat{\Theta}_{m}^{1}
, with image in
\Gamma^{m}
, on which the Taylor polynomial of the restriction
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
satisfies the
s_{m}
-vanishing condition. Namely, if
{\mathtt{a}}=(a_{21},\dots,a_{m1})\in\overline{B}^{m-1}(0,{\mathscr{K}}(r,n,m))
indicates the linear coefficients of
\gamma
and
{\widehat{\mathtt{J}}_{s_{m},\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}}
its
s_{m}
-truncation written in the adapted coordinates, by (
8.9
) one must have

({\mathtt{T}}_{0,{\mathtt{a}}}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m),{\mathtt{a}},{\widehat{\mathtt{J}}_{s_{m},\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})\in\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,s_{m},m))\ ,

(9.20)

that is, by (
5.37
),

\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{\ell\alpha}({\mathtt{T}}_{0,{\mathtt{a}}}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m),{\mathtt{a}},{\widehat{\mathtt{J}}_{s_{m},\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})=0\ ,\ \ \ell=1,\dots,m\ ,\ \ \lx@nobreakspace\alpha=0,\dots,s_{m}\ .

(9.21)

In particular, as
s_{m}\geq 2
, due to Theorem
5.1
and to (
7.5
), the equations in (
9.21
) for
\alpha=0,1
read

\begin{cases}h^{1}_{0}[v]=h^{1}_{0}[u_{1}]=\dots=h_{0}^{1}[u_{m}]=0\\
h_{0}^{2}[v,v]=h^{2}_{0}[v,u_{2}]=h^{2}_{0}[v,u_{m}]=0\\
\text{Span}(v,u_{2},\dots,u_{m})=\Gamma^{m}\end{cases}

(9.22)

where

v=u_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{m}a_{i1}u_{i}

(9.23)

is the anisotropic vector associated to the curve
\gamma
. Comparing (
9.6
) to (
9.22
), as the Hessian of

has only one null eigenvalue we see that the vectors

and

must be parallel, but then (
9.23
) yields

v=u_{1}\quad,\qquad a_{21}=\dots=a_{m1}=0\ ,

(9.24)

so that by the arguments of subsection (

) the coordinates adapted to the curve
\gamma
coincide with the original coordinates.

∎

We now recall that, due to Theorem
8.2
, the form of the set

Y^{1}_{1}(r,s_{m},m):=\Pi_{\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)}\left({\mathscr{N}}\left(\{\widehat{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathtt{i}}\alpha}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathtt{i}}\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,s_{m}\}\end{subarray}}\right)\bigcap\Upsilon^{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{1}^{1}(r,m)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(s))\right)

(9.25)

satisfying
20
20

20

The transformation
{\mathfrak{U}}^{1}
was introduced in Remark
5.7
.

X^{1}_{1}(r,s_{m},m)=\Pi_{\mathcal{P}(r,m)}\left(({\mathfrak{U}}^{1})^{-1}Y^{1}_{1}(r,s_{m},m)\right)

(9.26)

can be explicitly computed - starting from the relations determining
\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,s_{m},m))
- by the means of an algorithm involving only linear operations.
By this fact, the form of the set

\mathscr{Y}^{1}_{1}(r,s_{m},m):=Y^{1}_{1}(r,s_{m},m)\bigcap\{({\mathtt{p}}_{\mu},{\mathtt{a}})\in\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)|{\mathtt{a}}=0\}

(9.27)

can also be deduced explicitly starting from
\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,s_{m},m))
. Then, due to Lemma

and to (

), one has that

\overset{\textstyle\text{$h$ non-steep at $0$}\mathstrut}{\text{at order $s_{m}$ on $\Gamma^{m}$}}\ \Longrightarrow\ {\mathfrak{U}}^{1}({\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m),0)=({\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m),0)\in\mathscr{Y}^{1}_{1}(r,s_{m},m)\ .

(9.28)

Moreover, we observe the following facts:

1.

in section
7
, the explicit expression of set

introduced in Corollary B2 is obtained by injecting into the explicit expression for

\Upsilon^{1}(\widehat{Z}^{1}(r,s_{m},m)):={\mathscr{N}}\left\{{\mathtt{Q}}_{i,\alpha}({\mathtt{T}}_{0,{\mathtt{a}}}({\mathtt{P}}_{\mathtt{a}},r,m),{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{J}_{s_{m},\gamma,{\mathtt{a}}}})\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1,\dots,m\\
\alpha=0,\dots,s_{m}\end{subarray}}

given in Theorem

the explicit form of the coefficients of

) as free parameters of

.

2.

In the same way, for any

, one can inject in the expressions determining

the explicit form of the coefficients of
{\mathtt{T}}_{0,{\mathtt{a}}}(Q|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
, given in (
9.1
), with the vectors

considered as free parameters of

. The resulting subset is indicated by

{\mathscr{A}}_{1}(r,s_{m},n,m)\subset\mathcal{P}^{\star}(r,n)\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1}\times{\mathscr{V}}^{1}(m,n)\ .

By the arguments above, and by the fact that the form of

through linear algorithms, we have that the explicit expression of

can be found starting from the expressions determining

by only performing linear operations.

Furthermore, the above arguments together with (

) and with formula (

) yield that if system

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\begin{cases}(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})\in{\mathtt{U}}(m,n)\quad,\qquad\text{ Span }(u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{m})=\Gamma^{m}\in{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{1}(h,m,n)\\
({\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n),0,u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{m})\in{\mathscr{A}}_{1}(r,s_{m},n,m)\end{cases}\end{split}

(9.30)

has no solution, then
h
is steep at the origin with index
\alpha_{m}\leq 2s_{m}-1
on any subspace
\Gamma^{m}\in{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{1}(h,m,n)
.

9.2.2
Case of a subspace belonging to
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{2}(h,m,n)

As we did in paragraph
9.2.1
, we choose a basis
u_{1},\dots,u_{m}\in{\mathtt{U}}(m,n)
spanning
\Gamma^{m}
such that
u_{1}
coincides with a normalized eigenvector of the Hessian associated to one of the null eigenvalues. Then, we have

Lemma 9.3
.

If
h
is non-steep at the origin on
\Gamma^{m}\in{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{2}(h,m,n)
at a given order
s_{m}\geq 2
, up to suitably changing the order of the vectors
u_{1},\dots,u_{m}
one has

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\Sigma}^{1}(r,s_{m},m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{1}(r,m)\ .

(9.31)

Proof.

By (
9.10
), and by the fact that the hessian of
h|_{\Gamma^{m}}
has two or more null eigenvalues, we have that

\det\left(\begin{matrix}h^{2}_{0}[u_{2},u_{2}]&h^{2}_{0}[u_{2},u_{3}]&\dots&h^{2}_{0}[u_{2},u_{m}]\\
h^{2}_{0}[u_{3},u_{2}]&h^{2}_{0}[u_{3},u_{3}]&\dots&h^{2}_{0}[u_{3},u_{m}]\\
\dots\\
h^{2}_{0}[u_{m},u_{2}]&h^{2}_{0}[u_{m},u_{3}]&\dots&h^{2}_{0}[u_{m},u_{m}]\\
\end{matrix}\right)=0\ ,

(9.32)

hence
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{1}(r,m)
following definition (
8.20
).

Moreover,
h
is non-steep at the origin on
\Gamma^{m}
at a given order
s_{m}\geq 2
so that, by the discussions at section
6
and at paragraph
8.1
, one has

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\Sigma}(r,s_{m},m)

(9.33)

so that by the previous considerations we have

{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\Sigma}(r,s_{m},m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{1}(r,m)\ .

(9.34)

By the above expression and by (
8.9
), we have that there must exist
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
such that
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\Sigma}^{i}(r,s_{m},m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{1}(r,m)
. As it was already discussed in the proof of Theorem C1, if
{\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)\in\widehat{\Sigma}^{i}(r,s_{m},m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{1}(r,m)
then the vector
u_{i}
must satisfy (
9.16
). If
i=1
, there is nothing else to prove. If
i\neq 1
, it suffices to interchange the vector
u_{1}
with the vector
u_{i}
.
∎

Now, if for any
Q\in\mathcal{P}^{\star}(r,n)
we inject in the expressions
21
21

21

Contrary to the case studied in the previous paragraph, here these expressions cannot be found easily, in general.
determining the semi-algebraic subset
{\mathfrak{U}}^{1}\left(\left(\widehat{\Sigma}^{1}(r,s_{m},m)\bigcap{\mathsf{S}}_{2}^{1}(r,m)\right)\times\widehat{\vartheta}^{1}_{m}(1)\right)\subset\widehat{W}^{1}(r,m)
the explicit expression of the coefficients of the polynomial
{\mathtt{T}}_{0,{\mathtt{a}}}(Q|_{\Gamma^{m}},r,m)
, and we let the vectors

appearing in (
9.1
) vary in

, we obtain a set

{\mathscr{A}}_{2}(r,s_{m},n,m)\subset\mathcal{P}(r,n)\times\mathbb{R}^{m-1}\times{\mathscr{V}}^{1}(m,n)\ .

Moreover, by Lemma
9.3
, we have that if system

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\begin{cases}(a_{21},\dots,a_{m1})\in\overline{B}^{m-1}({\mathscr{K}})\\
(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})\in{\mathtt{U}}(m,n)\quad,\qquad v:=u_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{m}a_{i1}u_{i}\\
\text{ Span }(v,u_{2},\dots,u_{m})=\Gamma^{m}\in{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{2}(h,m,n)\\
({\mathtt{T}}_{0}(h,r,n),a_{21},\dots,a_{m1},v,u_{2},\dots,u_{m})\in{\mathscr{A}}_{2}(r,s_{m},n,m)\end{cases}\end{split}

(9.35)

has no solution, then
